B.3
Wasserstein Geometry of
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(M))

We recall that the WoW distance between
\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}\big(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})\big)
is defined as

\mathrm{W}_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})^{2}=\inf_{\Gamma\in\Pi(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})}\ \int\mathrm{W}_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu)\ \mathrm{d}\Gamma(\mu,\nu).

(50)

A natural question is to find the conditions under which this problem admits an OT map.
Emami & Pass 2025
showed it is the case for
\mathcal{M}
a compact connected Riemannian manifold, for absolutely continuous measures
w.r.t
a reference measure
\mathbb{P}_{0}
satisfying the following assumption:

Assumption B.2
.

•

\mathbb{P}_{0}
has no atoms

•

\mathbb{P}_{0}
satisfies the following integration by parts formula: for any
\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}\in\mathrm{Cyl}\big(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})\big)
, and any smooth vector field
w\in\Gamma(\mathcal{M},T\mathcal{M})
, there exists a measurable map
\mu\mapsto\nabla_{w}^{*}\mathcal{G}(\mu)\in T^{\mathrm{Der}}_{\mu}\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})
such that

\int_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})}\langle\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}\mathcal{F}(\mu),w\rangle_{L^{2}(\mu)}\cdot\mathcal{G}(\mu)\ \differential\mathbb{P}_{0}(\mu)=\int_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})}\mathcal{F}(\mu)\cdot\nabla_{w}^{*}\mathcal{G}(\mu)\ \differential\mathbb{P}_{0}(\mu).

(51)

•

\mathbb{P}_{0}
is quasi-invariant with respect to the action of the flows generated by smooth vector fields,
i.e.
for any smooth vector field
w\in\Gamma(\mathcal{M},T\mathcal{M})
,
\mathbb{P}_{0}
and
\mathbb{P}_{0}^{t,w}:=\Psi^{w,t}_{\#}\mathbb{P}_{0}
are mutually absolutely continuous for every
t\in\mathbb{R}
, and the Radon-Nikodym derivative

R_{r}^{w}=\frac{\differential\mathbb{P}_{0}^{t,w}\otimes\differential r}{\differential\mathbb{P}_{0}\otimes\differential r},\quad r\in\mathbb{R}

(52)

satisfies, for
\mathbb{P}_{0}
-a.e.
\mu
,
\mathcal{L}^{1}-\mathrm{essinf}_{r\in(s,t)}\ R_{r}^{w}(\mu)>0
for all
s,t\in\mathbb{R}
with
s\leq t
.

These assumptions were first proposed by
Schiavo 2020
, and we refer to
(
Schiavo 2020
)
for examples of measures satisfying them. By
(
Schiavo 2020
, Theorem 2.10)
, any
\mathbb{P}_{0}
satisfying
B.2
also satisfies the Rademacher property, which
Emami & Pass 2025
leveraged to show the existence of an OT map. In all the following, we fix a reference measure
\mathbb{P}_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}\big(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})\big)
satisfying
B.2
, and when there is no ambiguity, the “differentials” of a
\mathrm{W}_{2}
-Lipschitz function
U
will be denoted
DU
. Moreover, by
(
Schiavo 2020
, Theorem 2.10 (2))
, if
U\in\mathrm{Cyl}\big(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})\big)
, then its differential coincides with the usual Wasserstein gradient,
i.e.
,
DU=\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}U
.

Theorem B.3

(Theorem 13 in
(
Emami & Pass 2025
)
)
.

Let
\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}\big(\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{2,ac}}(\mathcal{M})\big)
such that
\mathbb{P}\ll\mathbb{P}_{0}
and
\mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}\big(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})\big)
. Then, there is a unique optimal plan
\Gamma
, which is of the form
\Gamma=(\mathrm{Id},\mathrm{T})_{\#}\mathbb{P}
with
\mathrm{T}:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})\to\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})
satisfying
\mathrm{T}_{\#}\mathbb{P}=\mathbb{Q}
. Moreover,
\mathrm{T}
is of the form
\mathrm{T}(\mu)=\exp\big(-D_{\bP_0} U(\mu)\big)_{\#}\mu
, where
U
is a (
\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{W}_{2}^{2}
-concave) Kantorovich potential for
\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q}
. In fact, for
\mathbb{P}
-a.e.
\mu
,
D_{\mathbb{P}_{0}}U(\mu)=\nabla\varphi_{\mu,\mathrm{T}(\mu)}
, where
\varphi_{\mu,\mathrm{T}(\mu)}
is a (
\frac{1}{2}d^{2}
-concave) Kantorovich potential for
\mu,\mathrm{T}(\mu)
.

Note that the last two statements on the form of
\mathrm{T}
are not part of the statement of the theorem in
(
Emami & Pass 2025
)
, but can be found in its proof.

Geodesics on
\mathcal{P}_{2}\big(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})\big)
.

We recall that a constant-speed geodesic between
\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}\big(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})\big)
is a curve
t\mapsto\mathbb{P}_{t}
defined on
[0,1]
, which satisfies
\mathbb{P}_{0}=\mathbb{P}
,
\mathbb{P}_{1}=\mathbb{Q}
and for all
s,t\in[0,1]
,
\mathrm{W}_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}(\mathbb{P}_{s},\mathbb{P}_{t})=|t-s|\mathrm{W}_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})
(see
e.g.

(
Santambrogio 2015
, Box 5.2)
).

As we work on manifolds, we introduce similarly as on
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})
a generalized inverse of the exponential map, which allows to characterize geodesics even when the optimal coupling is not unique. The multivalued inverse of the exponential map between
\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}\big(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})\big)
is then

\exp_{\mathbb{P}}^{-1}(\mathbb{Q})=\left\{\mathbb{\Gamma}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}\big(\mathcal{P}_{2}(T\mathcal{M})\big),\ \phi^{\mathcal{M}}_{\#}\mathbb{\Gamma}=\mathbb{P},\ \phi^{\exp}_{\#}\mathbb{\Gamma}=\mathbb{Q},\ \iint\|v\|_{x}^{2}\ \mathrm{d}\gamma(x,v)\mathrm{d}\mathbb{\Gamma}(\gamma)=\mathrm{W}_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})^{2}\right\},

(53)

where for any
\gamma\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(T\mathcal{M})
,
\phi^{\mathcal{M}}(\gamma)=\pi^{\mathcal{M}}_{\#}\gamma
and
\phi^{\exp}(\gamma)=\exp_{\#}\gamma
.

Proposition B.4
.

Let
\mathbb{\Gamma}\in\exp_{\mathbb{P}}^{-1}(\mathbb{Q})
. Then the curve
t\mapsto\mathbb{P}_{t}=\exp_{\phi^{\mathcal{M}}}\circ(t\phi^{\mathrm{v}})_{\#}\mathbb{\Gamma}
defines a geodesic between
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
.

Proof.

See
Section

C.12
.
∎

B.4
Differentiability on
\mathcal{P}_{2}\big(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})\big)

We recall that by
Section

3.2
, if
\mathbb{F}
is Wasserstein differentiable at
\mathbb{P}
, then the WoW gradient
\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}}\mathbb{F}(\mathbb{P})
satisfies for any
\mathbb{\Gamma}\in\exp_{\mathbb{P}}^{-1}(\mathbb{Q})
,

\mathbb{F}(\mathbb{Q})=\mathbb{F}(\mathbb{P})+\iint\langle\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}}\mathbb{F}(\mathbb{P})(\pi^{\mathcal{M}}_{\#}\gamma)(x),v\rangle_{x}\ \mathrm{d}\gamma(x,v)\mathrm{d}\mathbb{\Gamma}(\gamma)+o\big(\mathrm{W}_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})\big).

(54)

Using this formula, we now derive the gradient of potential and interaction energies.

Proposition B.5
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a compact and connected Riemannian manifold,
\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})\to\mathbb{R}
a twice Wasserstein differentiable functional with Hessian bounded in operator norm for all
\gamma\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(T\mathcal{M})
,
i.e.

\sup_{(x,v)\in\mathrm{supp}(\gamma),\ \|v\|_{x}=1}\ \|\mathrm{H}\mathcal{F}_{\gamma}(x,v)\|_{x}\leq L
,
and
\mathbb{F}(\mathbb{P})=\int\mathcal{F}(\mu)\ \mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}(\mu)
for
\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}\big(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})\big)
. Then,
\mathbb{F}
is Wasserstein differentiable, and its gradient is
\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}}\mathbb{F}(\mathbb{P})=\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}\mathcal{F}
.

Proof.

See
Section

C.13
.
∎

Proposition B.6
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a compact and connected Riemannian manifold,
\mathcal{W}:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})\to\mathbb{R}
be Wasserstein differentiable with respect to each of its argument and with bounded Hessian in operator norm for all
\gamma\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(T\mathcal{M})
as in
Proposition

B.5
. Let
\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}\big(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})\big)
and
\mathbb{F}(\mathbb{P})=\iint\mathcal{W}(\mu,\nu)\ \mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}(\mu)\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}(\nu)
. Then,
\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}}\mathbb{F}(\mathbb{P})(\mu)=\int\big(\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{2},1}\mathcal{W}(\mu,\nu)+\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{2},2}\mathcal{W}(\nu,\mu)\big)\ \mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}(\nu)
.

Proof.

See
Section

C.14
.
∎

Relation with first variation.

The gradients of the potential and interaction energies derived in the last two propositions are computed by showing that they satisfy the definition of the WoW gradients thanks to coupling arguments. Similarly to the case on
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})
, we expect that they can also be computed as the gradient of the first variation, which is a much simpler way to compute Wasserstein gradient of generic functionals. Let
\mathbb{F}:\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{ac}}\big(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})\big)\to\mathbb{R}
. Then the first variation
\frac{\delta\mathbb{F}}{\delta\mathbb{P}}(\mathbb{P}):\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})\to\mathbb{R}
at
\mathbb{P}
is defined as the unique function (up to a constant) satisfying

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\ \frac{\mathbb{F}(\mathbb{P}+\varepsilon\chi)-\mathbb{F}(\mathbb{P})}{\varepsilon}=\int\frac{\delta\mathbb{F}}{\delta\mathbb{P}}(\mathbb{P})\ \mathrm{d}\chi,

(55)

where
\int\mathrm{d}\chi=0
and
\mathbb{P}+\varepsilon\chi\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{ac}}\big(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})\big)
for
\varepsilon
small. Then, we expect that the WoW gradient of
\mathbb{F}
can be computed as

\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}}\mathbb{F}(\mathbb{P})=\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}\frac{\delta\mathbb{F}}{\delta\mathbb{P}}(\mathbb{P}).

(56)

We leave for future work to show formally this formula. Nonetheless, we verify that it holds for potential and interaction energies. Indeed, for
\mathbb{V}(\mathbb{P})=\int\mathcal{F}(\mu)\ \differential\mathbb{P}(\mu)
, we have
\frac{\delta\mathbb{V}}{\delta\mathbb{P}}(\mathbb{P})=\mathcal{F}
since

\mathbb{V}(\mathbb{P}+\varepsilon\chi)=\int\mathcal{F}(\mu)\differential\mathbb{P}(\mu)+\varepsilon\int\mathcal{F}(\mu)\differential\chi(\mu),

(57)

and thus the WoW gradient derived in
Proposition

B.5
coincides well with
\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}\frac{\delta\mathbb{V}}{\delta\mathbb{P}}(\mathbb{P})
. Similarly, for
\mathbb{W}(\mathbb{P})=\iint\mathcal{W}(\mu,\nu)\ \differential\mathbb{P}(\mu)\differential\mathbb{P}(\nu)
,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{W}(\mathbb{P}+t\chi)-\mathbb{W}(\mathbb{P})}{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{t}\left(\mathbb{W}(\mathbb{P})+t\iint\mathcal{W}(\mu,\nu)\ \mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}(\mu)\mathrm{d}\chi(\nu)+t\iint\mathcal{W}(\mu,\nu)\ \mathrm{d}\chi(\mu)\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}(\nu)\right.

(58)

\displaystyle\left.\quad+t^{2}\iint\mathcal{W}(\mu,\nu)\ \mathrm{d}\chi(\mu)\mathrm{d}\chi(\nu)-\mathbb{W}(\mathbb{P})\right)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow[t\to 0]{}\ \int\left(\int\mathcal{W}(\mu,\nu)\ \mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}(\mu)+\int\mathcal{W}(\nu,\mu)\ \mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}(\mu)\right)\ \mathrm{d}\chi(\nu).

Thus, the first variation is
\frac{\delta\mathbb{W}}{\delta\mathbb{P}}(\mathbb{P})(\nu)=\int\mathcal{W}(\mu,\nu)\ \mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}(\mu)+\int\mathcal{W}(\nu,\mu)\ \mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}(\mu)
, and its Wasserstein gradient coincides well with the WoW gradient derived in
Proposition

B.6
.

Relation with Euclidean gradient.

We provide now the analog of
Proposition

A.9
for WoW gradients, which we use in practice to compute them.

We fix here a number of classes
C>0
and a number of samples
n>0
, and we consider the class of (fully) discrete measures of
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
defined by

\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}}:=\frac{1}{C}\sum_{c=1}^{C}\delta_{\mu_{\mathbf{x}^{c}}},\quad\mathbf{x}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{C\times n},\quad\mu_{\mathbf{x}^{c}}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{x_{i}^{c}},\quad\mathbf{x}^{c}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{n}.

(59)

We also define the space

X:=\{\mathbf{x}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{C\times n}\,|\,\forall c,\ \mathbf{x}^{c}\notin\Delta_{n}\hbox{ and }\forall c\neq c^{\prime},\ \mu_{\mathbf{x}^{c}}\neq\mu_{\mathbf{x}^{c^{\prime}}}\}.

(60)

where
\Delta_{n}:=\{\mathbf{x}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{n}\,|\,\exists i\neq j,x_{i}=x_{j}\}
is the generalized diagonal of
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{n}
. Informally,
X
is the space of vectors
\mathbf{x}
such that the empirical measures
\mu_{\mathbf{x}^{c}}
in the support of
\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}}
are all distinct, and are each supported on
n
distinct points of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Proposition B.7
.

Let
\mathbb{F}:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\mapsto\mathbb{R}
a functional, and
F:(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{C\times n}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
such that for every
\mathbf{x}\in X
,
\mathbb{F}(\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}})=F(\mathbf{x})
. If
\mathbb{F}
is Wasserstein differentiable at
\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}}
and
F
is differentiable at
\mathbf{x}
for some
\mathbf{x}\in X
, then for every
c\in\{1,\ldots,C\}
and
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
,

\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}}\mathbb{F}(\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{x}})(\mu_{\mathbf{x}^{c}})(x^{c}_{i})=Cn\nabla_{c,i}F(\mathbf{x}).

(61)

Proof.

See
Section

C.15
.
∎

B.5
Convexity on
\mathcal{P}_{2}\big(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})\big)

In this section, we focus on
\mathcal{P}_{2}\big(\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{2,ac}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)
, and we show the convexity along generalized geodesics of potential energies and interaction energies. Hence,
Proposition

4.1
can be applied to these functionals.

We recall that on
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, a generalized geodesic between
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is of the form
t\mapsto\mu_{t}=\big((1-t)\pi^{1,2}+t\pi^{1,3}\big)_{\#}\gamma
with
\gamma\in\Pi(\eta,\mu,\nu)
such that
\pi^{1,2}_{\#}\gamma\in\Pi_{o}(\eta,\mu)
and
\pi^{1,3}_{\#}\gamma\in\Pi_{o}(\eta,\nu)
. Then a functional
\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}

\lambda
-convex along this curve satisfies for all
t\in[0,1]
,

\mathcal{F}(\mu_{t})\leq(1-t)\mathcal{F}(\mu)+t\mathcal{F}(\nu)-\frac{\lambda t(1-t)}{2}\mathrm{W}_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu).

(62)

When
\eta\in\mathcal{P}_{2,\mathrm{ac}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, by Brenier’s theorem, there are OT maps
\mathrm{T}_{\eta}^{\mu}
between
\eta
and
\mu
and
\mathrm{T}_{\eta}^{\nu}
between
\eta
and
\nu
, and the generalized geodesic translates as
\mu_{t}=\big((1-t)\mathrm{T}_{\eta}^{\mu}+t\mathrm{T}_{\eta}^{\nu}\big)_{\#}\eta
.

We define similarly a generalized geodesic between
\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{O}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}\big(\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{2,ac}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)
as
t\mapsto\mathbb{P}_{t}=\big(\big((1-t)\mathrm{T}_{\pi^{1}}^{\pi^{2}}+t\mathrm{T}_{\pi^{1}}^{\pi^{3}}\big)_{\#}\pi^{1}\big)_{\#}\Gamma
where
\Gamma\in\Pi(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{O})
,
\pi^{1,2}_{\#}\Gamma\in\Pi_{o}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})
and
\pi^{1,3}_{\#}\Gamma\in\Pi_{o}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{O})
. We provide sufficient conditions for potential and interaction energies to be
\lambda
-convex along generalized geodesics in
\mathcal{P}_{2}\big(\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{2,ac}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)
.

Proposition B.8
.

Let
\lambda\geq 0
and
\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
be
\lambda
-convex along generalized geodesics of
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then, the potential energy
\mathbb{F}(\mathbb{P})=\int\mathcal{F}(\mu)\differential\mathbb{P}(\mu)
is
\lambda
-convex along generalized geodesics on
\mathcal{P}_{2}\big(\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{2,ac}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)
.

Proof.

See
Section

C.16
.
∎

Proposition B.9
.

Let
\mathcal{W}:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
be joint convex along generalized geodesics of
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then, the interaction energy
\mathbb{W}(\mathbb{P})=\frac{1}{2}\iint\mathcal{W}(\mu,\nu)\ \differential\mathbb{P}(\mu)\differential\mathbb{P}(\nu)
is convex along generalized geodesics on
\mathcal{P}_{2}\big(\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{2,ac}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)
.

Proof.

See
Section

C.17
.
∎

We can also show that
\mathbb{F}:\mathbb{Q}\mapsto\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{W}_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}(\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{P})^{2}
is 1-convex along particular generalized geodesics, which have as anchor point
\mathbb{P}
.

Proposition B.10
.

Let
\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{O}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}\big(\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{2,ac}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)
. Define the generalized geodesic
t\mapsto\mathbb{P}_{t}=\big(\big((1-t)\mathrm{T}_{\pi^{1}}^{\pi^{2}}+t\mathrm{T}_{\pi^{1}}^{\pi^{3}}\big)_{\#}\pi^{1}\big)_{\#}\Gamma
where
\Gamma\in\Pi(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{O})
,
\pi^{1,2}_{\#}\Gamma\in\Pi_{o}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})
and
\pi^{1,3}_{\#}\Gamma\in\Pi_{o}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{O})
. Then
\mathbb{F}:\mathbb{Q}\mapsto\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{W}_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}(\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{P})^{2}
is 1-convex along this generalized geodesic,
i.e.
, it satisfies for all
t\in[0,1]
,

\mathbb{F}(\mathbb{P}_{t})\leq(1-t)\mathbb{F}(\mathbb{Q})+t\mathbb{F}(\mathbb{O})-\frac{t(1-t)}{2}\mathrm{W}_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}(\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{O})^{2}.

(63)

Proof.

See
Section

C.18
.
∎

In particular,
Proposition

B.10
is the main result allowing to show
Proposition

4.1
, which can be applied for
\lambda
-convex potential energies with
\lambda\geq 0
(or more generally, for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\frac{1}{\tau}+\lambda\geq 0
, see
(
Ambrosio et al. 2008
, Assumption 4.0.1 and Theorem 4.0.4)
), and for convex interaction energies.

Appendix C
Proofs

C.1
Proof of
Proposition

2.1

In the forthcoming proofs, we will make use of the following selection theorem, whose statement can be found in
(
Villani et al. 2009
, Chapter 5, Bibliographical notes)
:

Theorem C.1
.

If
f:A\mapsto B
is a Borel surjective map between Polish spaces (i.e. separable complete metric spaces), such that all the fibers
f^{-1}(y)
,
y\in B
are compact, then
f
admits a Borel right-inverse.

This allows us to prove

Lemma C.2
.

There exists a measurable selection
s:\mathcal{M}^{2}\mapsto T\mathcal{M}
of the map

f:\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}A=\{(x,v)\in T\mathcal{M},\ d\big(x,\exp_{x}(v)\big)=\|v\|_{x}\}&\rightarrow&\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M}\\
(x,v)&\mapsto&\big(x,\exp_{x}(v)\big).\end{array}\right.

(64)

Proof.

First,
A\subseteq T\mathcal{M}
is a Polish space, as a closed subset of
T\mathcal{M}
which is Polish. Second, for every
(x,y)\in\mathcal{M}^{2}
, the fiber
f^{-1}(x,y)
is compact. Indeed, if we let
\{(x_{n},v_{n})\}_{n=1}^{\infty}\subseteq f^{-1}(x,y)
, then we have for every
n
,
x_{n}=x
,
y=\exp_{x}(v_{n})
and
\|v_{n}\|_{x}=d(x,y)
, so by compactness of the spheres in the tangent space
T_{x}\mathcal{M}
, up to extracting a subsequence there exists
v\in T_{x}\mathcal{M}
such that
v_{n}\to v
, and by continuity
y=\exp_{x}(v)
so that
(x,v)\in f^{-1}(x,y)
. We can thus apply
Theorem

C.1
to
f
to deduce the existence of
s
.
∎

Now, we can prove
Proposition

2.1
. If
\gamma\in\exp^{-1}_{\mu}(\nu)
, we have
(\pi^{\mathcal{M}},\exp)_{\#}\gamma\in\Pi(\mu,\nu)
by definition of
\exp^{-1}_{\mu}(\nu)
. Furthermore

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{M}^{2}}d(x,y)^{2}\mathrm{d}(\pi^{\mathcal{M}},\exp)_{\#}\gamma(x,y)

\displaystyle=\int_{T\mathcal{M}}d(x,\exp_{x}(v))^{2}\mathrm{d}\gamma(x,v)

(65)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{T\mathcal{M}}\|v\|^{2}_{x}\mathrm{d}\gamma(x,v)=\mathrm{W}^{2}_{2}(\mu,\nu),

(66)

so this transport plan is optimal. In particular the inequality is an equality, and we find that
d(x,\exp_{x}(v))=\|v\|_{x}
for
\gamma
-a.e.
(x,v)
. The map is thus well defined. To show that it is surjective, take
\gamma\in\Pi_{o}(\mu,\nu)
, and set
\tilde{\gamma}:=s(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})_{\#}\gamma
, where
s:\mathcal{M}^{2}\mapsto T\mathcal{M}
is the selection map defined in
Lemma

C.2
. Then
\tilde{\gamma}\in\exp_{\mu}^{-1}(\nu)
. Indeed, by construction,
(\pi^{\mathcal{M}},\exp)_{\#}\tilde{\gamma}=\gamma\in\Pi(\mu,\nu)
, and also

\mathrm{W}_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu)=\int_{\mathcal{M}^{2}}d(x,y)^{2}\mathrm{d}\gamma(x,y)=\int_{T\mathcal{M}}d(x,\exp_{x}(v))^{2}\mathrm{d}\tilde{\gamma}(x,v)=\int_{T\mathcal{M}}\|v\|_{x}^{2}\mathrm{d}\tilde{\gamma}(x,v).

(67)

This proves the surjectivity of the map.

Now, assume that
\mu
is absolutely continuous, and that
\mathcal{M}
is compact and connected. Let
\gamma\in\exp^{-1}_{\mu}(\nu)
and
\tilde{\gamma}:=(\pi^{\mathcal{M}},\exp)_{\#}\gamma\in\Pi_{o}(\mu,\nu)
. By
Theorem

A.1
,
\tilde{\gamma}
is of the form
(\mathrm{Id},T)_{\#}\mu
where
T
is the unique optimal transport map from
\mu
to
\nu
. Furthermore
T
itself is of the form
T(x)=\exp_{x}(-\nabla\varphi(x))
where
\varphi
is a
c
-concave function
\mathcal{M}\to\mathbb{R}
(in fact a Kantorovich potential for the pair
\mu,\nu
). Furthermore, we have, for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in\mathcal{M}
,
T(x)
belongs to

\partial^{c}\varphi(x):=\{y\in\mathcal{M},\ \varphi(x)+\varphi^{c}(y)=\frac{1}{2}d(x,y)^{2}\}.

(68)

This implies, by
(
Gigli 2011
, Theorem 1.8)
, that for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in\mathcal{M}
,
\exp^{-1}_{x}(T(x))\subseteq-\partial^{+}\varphi(x)
, where
\partial^{+}\varphi(x)
is the superdifferential of
\varphi
. However, since
\mathcal{M}
is compact,
\varphi
is Lipschitz and is thus differentiable almost everywhere, so that
\partial^{+}\varphi(x)=\{\nabla\varphi(x)\}
almost everywhere (in particular this holds
\mu
-a.e. as
\mu
is absolutely continuous). From this, we conclude that for
\gamma
-a.e.
(x,v)
, we have

T(x)=\exp_{x}(v)
with
\exp^{-1}_{x}(T(x))=\{-\nabla\varphi(x)\}
, so that
v=-\nabla\varphi(x)
. Thus, we have proved that

\gamma=(\mathrm{Id},-\nabla\varphi)_{\#}\mu.

(69)

This finishes the proof. ∎

C.2
Proof of
Proposition

3.1

We first state and prove another selection result:

Lemma C.3
.

There exists a measurable selection
s:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})^{2}\mapsto\mathcal{P}_{2}(T\mathcal{M})
of the map

f:A=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}\{\gamma\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(T\mathcal{M})|\gamma\in\exp_{\pi^{\mathcal{M}}_{\#}\gamma}^{-1}(\exp_{\#}\gamma)\}&\rightarrow&\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})\\
\gamma&\mapsto&(\pi^{\mathcal{M}}_{\#}\gamma,\exp_{\#}\gamma).\end{array}\right.

(70)

Proof.

We first prove that
A\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{2}(T\mathcal{M})
is a Polish space. All we need to do is to prove that it is closed : indeed, since
T\mathcal{M}
is a connected Riemannian manifold (with the Sasaki metric),
(\mathcal{P}_{2}(T\mathcal{M}),\mathrm{W}_{2})
is a Polish space. (See
(
Ambrosio et al. 2008
, Proposition 7.1.5)
. Similarly
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})
is a Polish space.) Note first that if
\gamma\in A
, it is supported on the compact set
K=\{(x,v)\in T\mathcal{M},\ \|v\|_{x}\leq\mathrm{diam}(\mathcal{M})\}\subseteq T\mathcal{M}
, as
\|v\|_{x}=d(x,\exp_{x}(v))

\gamma
-almost everywhere. Let
\{\gamma_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}\subseteq A
converging to
\gamma\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(T\mathcal{M})
in the
\mathrm{W}_{2}
metric. Then
\gamma
is also supported on
K
, and for every
n
, since
\gamma_{n}\in\exp^{-1}_{\pi^{\mathcal{M}}_{\#}\gamma_{n}}(\exp_{\#}\gamma_{n})
, we have

\int\|v\|^{2}_{x}\differential\gamma_{n}(x,v)=\mathrm{W}_{2}^{2}(\pi^{\mathcal{M}}_{\#}\gamma_{n},\exp_{\#}\gamma_{n}).

(71)

Letting
n\to\infty
, we find

\int\|v\|^{2}_{x}\differential\gamma(x,v)=\mathrm{W}_{2}^{2}(\pi^{\mathcal{M}}_{\#}\gamma,\exp_{\#}\gamma).

(72)

Indeed,
\mathrm{W}_{2}(\gamma_{n},\gamma)\to 0
implies weak convergence of
\gamma_{n}
to
\gamma
, and thus of
\pi^{\mathcal{M}}_{\#}\gamma_{n}
and
\exp_{\#}\gamma_{n}
to respectively
\pi^{\mathcal{M}}_{\#}\gamma
and
\exp_{\#}\gamma
, and since
\mathcal{M}
is compact, weak convergence on
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})
is the same as
\mathrm{W}_{2}
convergence, which in turn implies the convergence of the right-hand side. The left-hand side converges similarly in virtue of the weak convergence of
\gamma_{n}
to
\gamma
(recall that they are supported on the compact set
K
on which the function
(x,v)\to\|v\|^{2}_{x}
is bounded). Therefore, (
72
) implies that
\gamma\in\exp^{-1}_{\pi^{\mathcal{M}}_{\#}\gamma}(\exp_{\#}\gamma)
, so that
\gamma\in A
, and
A
is thus closed in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(T\mathcal{M})
, and a Polish space.

Now, we prove that the fibers of
f
are compact. Let
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})
and
\{\gamma_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}\subseteq f^{-1}(\mu,\nu)
. Since they are supported on
K
, and
\mathcal{P}(K)
is compact by compactness of
K
, there exists
\gamma\in\mathcal{P}(K)\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{2}(T\mathcal{M})
such that, up to extracting a subsequence,
\gamma_{n}
converges to
\gamma
, both weakly and in the
\mathrm{W}_{2}
metric. We then check as above that
\gamma\in A
, with
\pi^{\mathcal{M}}_{\#}\gamma=\mu
and
\exp_{\#}\gamma=\nu
. This proves that the fibers of
f
are compact. Now, the existence of
s
follows again from
Theorem

C.1
.
∎

The proof of
Proposition

3.1
is pretty much similar to the previous one. If
\mathbb{\Gamma}\in\exp^{-1}_{\mathbb{P}}(\mathbb{Q})
, we have
(\phi^{\mathcal{M}},\phi^{\exp})_{\#}\mathbb{\Gamma}\in\Pi(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})
by definition of
\exp^{-1}_{\mathbb{P}}(\mathbb{Q})
. Furthermore,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})^{2}}\mathrm{W}_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu)\mathrm{d}(\phi^{\mathcal{M}},\phi^{\exp})_{\#}\mathbb{\Gamma}(\mu,\nu)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(T\mathcal{M})}\mathrm{W}_{2}^{2}(\pi^{\mathcal{M}}_{\#}\gamma,\exp_{\#}\gamma)\mathrm{d}\mathbb{\Gamma}(\gamma)

(73)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(T\mathcal{M})}\int_{\mathcal{M}^{2}}d(x,y)^{2}\mathrm{d}(\pi_{\mathcal{M}},\exp)_{\#}\gamma(x,y)\mathrm{d}\mathbb{\Gamma}(\gamma)

(74)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(T\mathcal{M})}\int_{T\mathcal{M}}d(x,\exp_{x}(v))^{2}\mathrm{d}\gamma(x,v)\mathrm{d}\mathbb{\Gamma}(\gamma)

(75)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(T\mathcal{M})}\int_{T\mathcal{M}}\|v\|_{x}^{2}\mathrm{d}\gamma(x,v)\mathrm{d}\mathbb{\Gamma}(\gamma)=\mathrm{W}_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})^{2},

(76)

so this transport plan is optimal. In particular all the inequalities are equalities, and we find that for
\mathbb{\Gamma}
-a.e.
\gamma
,
\mathrm{W}_{2}^{2}(\pi^{\mathcal{M}}_{\#}\gamma,\exp_{\#}\gamma)=\int\|v\|_{x}^{2}\mathrm{d}\gamma(x,v)
hence
\gamma\in\exp_{\pi^{\mathcal{M}}_{\#}\gamma}^{-1}(\exp_{\#}\gamma)
. The map is thus well defined. To show that it is surjective, take
\mathbb{\Gamma}\in\Pi_{o}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})
, and set
\tilde{\mathbb{\Gamma}}:=s(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})_{\#}\mathbb{\Gamma}
, where
s:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})^{2}\mapsto\mathcal{P}_{2}(T\mathcal{M})
is the selection map defined in
Lemma

C.3
.
Then
\tilde{\mathbb{\Gamma}}\in\exp_{\mathbb{P}}^{-1}(\mathbb{Q})
as, by construction,
(\phi^{\mathcal{M}},\phi^{\exp})_{\#}\tilde{\mathbb{\Gamma}}=\mathbb{\Gamma}\in\Pi(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})
, and also

\displaystyle\mathrm{W}_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})^{2}}\mathrm{W}_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu)\differential\mathbb{\Gamma}(\mu,\nu)

(77)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(T\mathcal{M})}\mathrm{W}_{2}^{2}(\pi^{\mathcal{M}}_{\#}\gamma,\exp_{\#}\gamma)\differential\tilde{\mathbb{\Gamma}}(\gamma)

(78)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(T\mathcal{M})}\int_{T\mathcal{M}}\|v\|^{2}_{x}\differential\gamma(x,v)\differential\tilde{\mathbb{\Gamma}}(\gamma).

(79)

This proves the surjectivity of the map.

Now, assume that
\mathbb{P}
is absolutely continuous with respect to
\mathbb{P}_{0}
, and that
\mathcal{M}
is compact and connected. Let
\mathbb{\Gamma}\in\exp^{-1}_{\mathbb{P}}(\mathbb{Q})
and
\tilde{\mathbb{\Gamma}}:=(\phi^{\mathcal{M}},\phi^{\exp})_{\#}\mathbb{\Gamma}\in\Pi_{o}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})
. By
(
Emami & Pass 2025
, Theorem 13)
,
\tilde{\mathbb{\Gamma}}
is of the form
(\mathrm{Id},T)_{\#}\mathbb{P}
where
T
is the unique optimal transport map from
\mathbb{P}
to
\mathbb{Q}
. Furthermore, for
\mathbb{\Gamma}
-a.e.
\gamma
, we have
\gamma\in\exp^{-1}_{\mu_{\gamma}}(\nu_{\gamma})
, with
\mu_{\gamma}:=\pi^{\mathcal{M}}_{\#}\gamma
, and
\nu_{\gamma}:=\exp_{\#}\gamma
. Since
\tilde{\mathbb{\Gamma}}=(\mathrm{Id},T)_{\#}\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{P}
is concentrated on absolutely continuous measures (as
\mathbb{P}\ll\mathbb{P}_{0}
), we also have that for
\mathbb{\Gamma}
-a.e.
\gamma
,
\mu_{\gamma}
is absolutely continuous and
\nu_{\gamma}=T(\mu_{\gamma})
. Thus, by
Proposition

2.1
, we have
\gamma=(\mathrm{Id},-\nabla\varphi_{\mu_{\gamma},T(\mu_{\gamma})})_{\#}\mu_{\gamma}=\big(\mu\to(\mathrm{Id},-\nabla\varphi_{\mu,T(\mu)})\big)\circ\phi^{\mathcal{M}}(\gamma)
for
\mathbb{\Gamma}
-a.e.
\gamma
. Therefore, we have proved that

\mathbb{\Gamma}=\big(\mu\to(\mathrm{Id},-\nabla\varphi_{\mu,T(\mu)})\big)_{\#}\phi^{\mathcal{M}}_{\#}\mathbb{\Gamma}=\big(\mu\to(\mathrm{Id},-\nabla\varphi_{\mu,T(\mu)})_{\#}\mu\big)_{\#}\mathbb{P}.

(80)

This finishes the proof. ∎

Remark C.4
.

As a side note, there exists a more explicit expression for the unique
\mathbb{\Gamma}\in\exp^{-1}_{\mathbb{P}}(\mathbb{Q})
. Let indeed
U:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})\mapsto\mathbb{R}
be a Kantorovich potential for
\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q}
(
i.e.
a
\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{W}_{2}^{2}
-concave function solving the dual problem). Then, by the same reasoning as in the proof of
(
Emami & Pass 2025
, Theorem 13)
, for
\mathbb{P}
-almost every
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})
, we have
\nabla\varphi_{\mu,T(\mu)}=DU(\mu)
. Thus, we have

\mathbb{\Gamma}=\big(\mu\to(\mathrm{Id},-DU(\mu))_{\#}\mu\big)_{\#}\mathbb{P}.

(81)

C.3
Proof of
Proposition

3.7

The proof is inspired from
(
Erbar 2010
, Proposition 2.5)
and
(
Ambrosio et al. 2008
, Theorem 8.3.1)
.

First, fix
\varphi\in\mathrm{Cyl}\big(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})\big)
, such that
\varphi(\mu):=F\left(\int V_{1}\differential\mu,\ldots,\int V_{m}\differential\mu\right)
with
F\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{m})
and
V_{1},\ldots,V_{m}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathcal{M})
. Let us define
H:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})\to\mathbb{R}
as

H(\mu,\nu):=\begin{cases}\frac{|\varphi(\mu)-\varphi(\nu)|}{\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu,\nu)}&\hbox{ if }\mu\neq\nu\\
\|\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}\varphi(\mu)\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}&\hbox{ if }\mu=\nu.\end{cases}

(82)

We show in
Lemma

C.5
that
H
is upper semicontinuous. We want to prove that
t\to\mathbb{P}_{t}(\varphi)=\int\varphi\differential\mathbb{P}_{t}
is absolutely continuous and bound its metric derivative. For every
s,t\in I
, let
\Gamma_{s,t}\in\Pi_{o}(\mathbb{P}_{s},\mathbb{P}_{t})
. Then

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|h|}|\mathbb{P}_{s+h}(\varphi)-\mathbb{P}_{s}(\varphi)|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{|h|}\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{M})^{2}}|\varphi(\mu)-\varphi(\nu)|\ \mathrm{d}\Gamma_{s+h,s}(\mu,\nu)

(83)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{|h|}\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{M})^{2}}\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu,\nu)H(\mu,\nu)\ \mathrm{d}\Gamma_{s+h,s}(\mu,\nu)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathrm{W}_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}(\mathbb{P}_{s+h},\mathbb{P}_{s})}{|h|}\sqrt{\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{M})^{2}}H^{2}(\mu,\nu)\ \mathrm{d}\Gamma_{s+h,s}(\mu,\nu)}.

Now, we have
\Gamma_{s+h,s}\rightharpoonup(\mathrm{Id},\mathrm{Id})_{\#}\mathbb{P}_{s}
when
h\to 0
, so, since
H
is upper semicontinuous, by
(
Ambrosio et al. 2008
, Lemma 5.1.7)
, we have

\limsup_{h\to 0}\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{M})^{2}}H^{2}(\mu,\nu)\ \mathrm{d}\Gamma_{s+h,s}(\mu,\nu)\leq\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{M})}H^{2}(\mu,\mu)\ \mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{s}(\mu)=\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{M})}\|\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}\varphi(\mu)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\ \mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{s}(\mu),

(84)

and thus
s\mapsto\mathbb{P}_{s}(\varphi)
is absolutely continuous, with metric derivative bounded from above by

|\mathbb{P}^{\prime}|(s)\|\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}\varphi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{s},T\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})))}.

(85)

Let now
\varphi\in\mathrm{Cyl}\big(I\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})\big)
,
Q=I\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})
, and
\lambda=\int_{I}\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{t}\mathrm{d}t
. Then, for any interval
J\subseteq I
such that
\mathrm{spt}(\varphi)\subseteq J\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})
,

\displaystyle\left|\int_{Q}\partial_{t}\varphi\ \mathrm{d}\lambda\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\int_{Q}\frac{\varphi(t,\mu)-\varphi(t-h,\mu)}{h}\ \mathrm{d}\lambda(t,\mu)\right|

(86)

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{h\to 0^{+}}\left|\int_{J}\frac{\mathbb{P}_{t}(\varphi_{t})-\mathbb{P}_{t+h}(\varphi_{t})}{h}\ \mathrm{d}t\right|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{J}\limsup_{h\to 0^{+}}\frac{|\mathbb{P}_{t}(\varphi_{t})-\mathbb{P}_{t+h}(\varphi_{t})|}{|h|}\ \differential t

\displaystyle\leq\int_{J}|\mathbb{P}^{\prime}|(t)\|\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}\varphi_{t}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{t})}\ \differential t

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\int_{J}|\mathbb{P}^{\prime}|^{2}(t)\ \differential t}\sqrt{\int_{J}\|\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}\varphi_{t}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{t})}^{2}\ \differential t}.

From this, we infer that the linear form

L(\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}\varphi):=-\int_{Q}\partial_{t}\varphi\ \differential\lambda

(87)

is well-defined and Lipschitz continuous. In particular, there exists
v\in\overline{\{\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}\varphi,\ \varphi\in\mathrm{Cyl}\big(I\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})\big)\}}^{L^{2}(\lambda,T\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M}))}
such that for every
\varphi\in\mathrm{Cyl}\big(I\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})\big)
,

L(\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}\varphi)=\langle v,\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}\varphi\rangle_{L^{2}(\lambda)}=\int_{I}\int_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})}\langle v_{t}(\mu),\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}\varphi(\mu)\rangle_{L^{2}(\mu)}\ \differential\mathbb{P}_{t}(\mu)\differential t,

(88)

and we have the continuity equation.

Moreover, for a.e.
t\in I
,
v_{t}\in\overline{\{\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}\varphi,\ \varphi\in\mathrm{Cyl}\big(I\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})\big)\}}^{L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{t},T\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M}))}
, and for every interval
J\subseteq I
, there exists a sequence
(\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}\varphi_{n})_{n}
supported in
J\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})
such that
\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}\varphi_{n}\to v_{t}\mathbb{1}_{J}
. For all
n
,

L(\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}\varphi_{n})\leq\left(\int_{J}|\mathbb{P}^{\prime}|(t)^{2}\ \mathrm{d}t\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{J}\|\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}\varphi_{n}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{t},T\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M}))}^{2}\ \mathrm{d}t\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

(89)

and letting
n\to\infty
, we find

\displaystyle\int_{J}\|v_{t}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{t},T\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M}))}^{2}\ \mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{J}|\mathbb{P}^{\prime}|(t)^{2}\ \mathrm{d}t\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{J}\|v_{t}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{t},T\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M}))}^{2}\ \mathrm{d}t\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

(90)

\displaystyle\int_{J}\|v_{t}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{t},T\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M}))}^{2}\ \mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\int_{J}|\mathbb{P}^{\prime}|(t)^{2}\ \mathrm{d}t.

We thus conclude that for a.e.
t\in I
,

\|v_{t}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{t},T\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M}))}\leq|\mathbb{P}^{\prime}|(t).\qed

(91)

We now show that
H
is upper semicontinuous.

Lemma C.5
.

Let
\varphi\in\mathrm{Cyl}\big(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})\big)
. The function
H:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})\to\mathbb{R}
defined as

H(\mu,\nu):=\begin{cases}\frac{|\varphi(\mu)-\varphi(\nu)|}{W_{2}(\mu,\nu)}&\hbox{ if }\mu\neq\nu\\
\|\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}\varphi(\mu)\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}&\hbox{ if }\mu=\nu,\end{cases}

(92)

is upper semicontinuous.

Proof.

We want to show that the function
H:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})\to\mathbb{R}
defined by

H(\mu,\nu):=\begin{cases}\frac{|\varphi(\mu)-\varphi(\nu)|}{W_{2}(\mu,\nu)}&\hbox{ if }\mu\neq\nu\\
\|\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}\varphi(\mu)\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}&\hbox{ if }\mu=\nu\end{cases}

(93)

is upper semicontinuous. Let
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})
, and let
(\mu_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
be the constant speed geodesic from
\mu
to
\nu
with velocity field
v_{t}\in L^{2}(\mu_{t})
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\varphi(\nu)-\varphi(\mu)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{d}{dt}\varphi(\mu_{t})\ \differential t

(94)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{d}{dt}F\left(\int V_{1}\differential\mu_{t},\ldots,\int V_{m}\differential\mu_{t}\right)\ \differential t

(95)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{\partial F}{\partial x_{i}}\left(\int V_{1}\differential\mu_{t},\ldots,\int V_{m}\differential\mu_{t}\right)\frac{d}{dt}\int V_{i}\differential\mu_{t}\ \differential t

(96)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{\partial F}{\partial x_{i}}\left(\int V_{1}\differential\mu_{t},\ldots,\int V_{m}\differential\mu_{t}\right)\int\langle\nabla V_{i},v_{t}\rangle\ \differential\mu_{t}\differential t

(97)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\langle\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}\varphi(\mu_{t}),v_{t}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mu_{t})}\differential t

(98)

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\int_{0}^{1}\|v_{t}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu_{t})}\ \differential t}\sqrt{\int_{0}^{1}\|\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}\varphi(\mu_{t})\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu_{t})}\differential t}

(99)

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu,\nu)\sqrt{\int_{0}^{1}\|\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}\varphi(\mu_{t})\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu_{t})}\differential t}.

(100)

Hence,

H(\mu,\nu)\leq\sqrt{\int_{0}^{1}\|\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}\varphi(\mu_{t})\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu_{t})}\differential t}<\infty

(101)

as the right-hand side is finite because the
F,V_{i}
have compact support. Note that this inequality is also true when
\mu=\nu
as
\mu_{t}=\mu
for every
t
.

Furthermore, notice that the map
f:\mu\mapsto\|\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}\varphi(\mu)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}
is continuous. Indeed, we can check that we have

f(\mu)=\int G\left(\int V_{1}\differential\mu,\ldots,\int V_{m}\differential\mu,x\right)\differential\mu(x)

(102)

for some Lipschitz function
G
with Lipschitz constant
L
, so that if
\mu^{n}\rightharpoonup\mu
, we have

\displaystyle|f(\mu^{n})-f(\mu)|

\displaystyle=\left|\int G\left(\int V_{1}\differential\mu^{n},\ldots,\int V_{m}\differential\mu^{n},x\right)\differential\mu^{n}(x)-\int G\left(\int V_{1}\differential\mu,\ldots,\int V_{m}\differential\mu,x\right)\differential\mu(x)\right|

(103)

\displaystyle\leq\left|\int G\left(\int V_{1}\differential\mu^{n},\ldots,\int V_{m}\differential\mu^{n},x\right)\differential\mu^{n}(x)-\int G\left(\int V_{1}\differential\mu,\ldots,\int V_{m}\differential\mu,x\right)\differential\mu^{n}(x)\right|

(104)

\displaystyle+\left|\int G\left(\int V_{1}\differential\mu,\ldots,\int V_{m}\differential\mu,x\right)\differential\mu^{n}(x)-\int G\left(\int V_{1}\differential\mu,\ldots,\int V_{m}\differential\mu,x\right)\differential\mu(x)\right|

(105)

\displaystyle\leq L\sum_{i=1}^{m}\left|\int V_{i}\differential(\mu^n-\mu)\right|+\left|\int G\left(\int V_{1}\differential\mu,\ldots,\int V_{m}\differential\mu,x\right)\differential(\mu^n-\mu)(x)\right|\rightarrow 0.

(106)

Now, if
\mu^{n}\rightharpoonup\mu
and
\nu^{n}\rightharpoonup\mu
, then
\mu^{n}_{t}\rightharpoonup\mu
for every
t
(indeed
\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{t}^{n},\mu)\leq\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu_{t}^{n},\mu^{n})+\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu^{n},\mu)=t\mathrm{W}_{2}(\nu^{n},\mu^{n})+\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu^{n},\mu)\to 0
), and thus by what precedes
\|\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}\varphi(\mu^{n}_{t})\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu_{t})}\mapsto\|\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}\varphi(\mu)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}
for every
t
. Therefore, by (
101
), we deduce

\limsup_{n}H(\mu^{n},\nu^{n})\leq\|\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}\varphi(\mu)\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=H(\mu,\mu).

(107)

This proves the upper semicontinuity of
H
.
∎

C.4
Proof of
Proposition

3.8

Let
\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}\big(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})\big)
, and define
\mathcal{P}_{2}\big(\mathcal{P}_{2}(T\mathcal{M})\big)_{\mathbb{P}}:=\{\mathbb{\Gamma}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}\big(\mathcal{P}_{2}(T\mathcal{M})\big),\phi^{\mathcal{M}}_{\#}\mathbb{\Gamma}=\mathbb{P}\}
. Fix
\mathbb{\Gamma}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}\big(\mathcal{P}_{2}(T\mathcal{M})\big)_{\mathbb{P}}
, we define

\|\mathbb{\Gamma}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{P}}:=\iint\|v\|^{2}_{x}\differential\gamma(x,v)\differential\mathbb{\Gamma}(\gamma).

(108)

By the disintegration theorem (see for example
(
Ambrosio et al. 2008
, Theorem 5.3.1)
), there exists a
\mathbb{P}
-a.e. unique family of probability measures
(\mathbb{\Gamma}_{\mu})_{\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})}
such that
\mathbb{\Gamma}_{\mu}
is supported on
\mathcal{P}_{2}(T\mathcal{M})_{\mu}
and, for every measurable test function
f:\mathcal{P}_{2}(T\mathcal{M})\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{+}
,

\int f(\gamma)\differential\mathbb{\Gamma}(\gamma)=\iint f(\gamma)\differential\mathbb{\Gamma}_{\mu}(\gamma)\differential\mathbb{P}(\mu).

(109)

We can use this family of measures to define the barycentric projection of
\mathbb{\Gamma}
:

Definition C.6
.

The barycentric projection of
\mathbb{\Gamma}
is the vector field
\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{\Gamma})\in L^{2}(\mathbb{P},T\mathcal{M})
defined by

\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{\Gamma})(\mu):=\int\mathcal{B}(\gamma)\differential\mathbb{\Gamma}_{\mu}(\gamma)\in L^{2}(\mu,T\mathcal{M}).

(110)

Note that we work here in the space
L^{2}(\mathbb{P},T\mathcal{M})
, which is defined in
Section

B.1
. It is a larger space than
L^{2}(\mathbb{P},T\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M}))
, with which it should not be confused. The barycentric projection satisfies the following properties:

Proposition C.7
.

For every
\xi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{P},T\mathcal{M})
, it holds

\iint\langle\xi(\pi^{\mathcal{M}}_{\#}\gamma)(x),v\rangle_{x}\differential\gamma(x,v)\mathbb{\Gamma}(\gamma)=\int\langle\xi(\mu),\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{\Gamma})(\mu)\rangle_{L^{2}(\mu)}\differential\mathbb{P}(\mu)=\langle\xi,\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{\Gamma})\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P})}.

(111)

Furthermore
\|\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{\Gamma})\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P})}\leq\|\mathbb{\Gamma}\|_{\mathbb{P}}
.

Proof.

For every
\xi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{P},T\mathcal{M})
, we have

\displaystyle\iint\langle\xi(\pi^{\mathcal{M}}_{\#}\gamma)(x),v\rangle_{x}\differential\gamma(x,v)\mathbb{\Gamma}(\gamma)

\displaystyle=\iiint\langle\xi(\pi^{\mathcal{M}}_{\#}\gamma)(x),v\rangle_{x}\differential\gamma(x,v)\differential\mathbb{\Gamma}_{\mu}(\gamma)\differential\mathbb{P}(\mu)

(112)

\displaystyle=\iiint\langle\xi(\mu)(x),v\rangle_{x}\differential\gamma(x,v)\differential\mathbb{\Gamma}_{\mu}(\gamma)\differential\mathbb{P}(\mu)

(113)

\displaystyle=\iint\langle\xi(\mu),\mathcal{B}(\gamma)\rangle_{L^{2}(\mu)}\differential\mathbb{\Gamma}_{\mu}(\gamma)\differential\mathbb{P}(\mu)

(114)

\displaystyle=\int\langle\xi(\mu),\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{\Gamma})(\mu)\rangle_{L^{2}(\mu)}\differential\mathbb{P}(\mu)=\langle\xi,\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{\Gamma})\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P})}.

(115)

Furthermore,

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{\Gamma})\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P})}

\displaystyle=\iint\left\|\int\mathcal{B}(\gamma)(x)\differential\mathbb{\Gamma}_{\mu}(\gamma)\right\|^{2}_{x}\differential\mu(x)\differential\mathbb{P}(\mu)

(116)

\displaystyle\leq\iiint\|\mathcal{B}(\gamma)(x)\|^{2}_{x}\differential\mathbb{\Gamma}_{\mu}(\gamma)\differential\mu(x)\differential\mathbb{P}(\mu)

(117)

\displaystyle\leq\iint\|\mathcal{B}(\gamma)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\differential\mathbb{\Gamma}_{\mu}(\gamma)\differential\mathbb{P}(\mu)

(118)

\displaystyle\leq\iiint\|v\|^{2}_{x}\differential\gamma(x,v)\differential\mathbb{\Gamma}_{\mu}(\gamma)\differential\mathbb{P}(\mu)

(119)

\displaystyle\leq\iint\|v\|^{2}_{x}\differential\gamma(x,v)\differential\mathbb{\Gamma}(\gamma)=\|\mathbb{\Gamma}\|_{\mathbb{P}}^{2},

(120)

where we used
\|\mathcal{B}(\gamma)\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}\leq\|\gamma\|_{\mu}^{2}=\int\|v\|^{2}_{x}\differential\gamma(x,v)
to obtain the fourth line.
∎

We now show
Proposition

3.8
.

Assume by contradiction that
\xi
is not a strong subdifferential of
\mathbb{F}
at
\mathbb{P}
. Then, there exists
\delta>0
and a sequence
\{\mathbb{\Gamma}_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{2}\big(\mathcal{P}_{2}(T\mathcal{M})\big)_{\mathbb{P}}
such that
\varepsilon_{n}:=\|\mathbb{\Gamma}_{n}\|_{\mathbb{P}}\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}0
, and, for every
n
,

\mathbb{F}(\mathbb{P}_{n})-\mathbb{F}(\mathbb{P})-\iint\langle\xi(\pi^{\mathcal{M}}_{\#}\gamma),v\rangle_{x}\differential\gamma(x,v)\differential\mathbb{\Gamma}_{n}(\gamma)\leq-\delta\varepsilon_{n}

(121)

with
\mathbb{P}_{n}:=\phi^{\exp}_{\#}\mathbb{\Gamma}_{n}
. Now, for every
n
, fix
\mathbb{\Upsilon}_{n}\in\exp^{-1}_{\mathbb{P}}(\mathbb{P}_{n})
. Since
\xi\in\partial^{-}\mathbb{F}(\mathbb{P})
, there exists
N>0
such that for every
n>N
,

\mathbb{F}(\mathbb{P}_{n})-\mathbb{F}(\mathbb{P})\geq\iint\langle\xi(\pi^{\mathcal{M}}_{\#}\gamma)(x),v\rangle_{x}\differential\gamma(x,v)\differential\mathbb{\Upsilon}_{n}(\gamma)-\frac{\delta}{2}\mathrm{W}_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}(\mathbb{P}_{n},\mathbb{P}).

(122)

Denoting
\Psi_{n}:=\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{\Gamma}_{n})
and
\Phi_{n}:=\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{\Upsilon}_{n})
, we have, combining (
121
) and (
122
), that

\langle\xi,\Psi_{n}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P})}-\delta\varepsilon_{n}\geq\langle\xi,\Phi_{n}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P})}-\frac{\delta}{2}\mathrm{W}_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}(\mathbb{P}_{n},\mathbb{P}).

(123)

Furthermore, we have
\mathrm{W}_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}(\mathbb{P}_{n},\mathbb{P})\leq\varepsilon_{n}
, since

\displaystyle\mathrm{W}_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}(\mathbb{P}_{n},\mathbb{P})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\int\mathrm{W}_{2}^{2}(\pi^{\mathcal{M}}_{\#}\gamma,\exp_{\#}\gamma)\differential\mathbb{\Gamma}_{n}(\gamma)

(124)

\displaystyle\leq\iint d^{2}(x,\exp_{x}(v))\differential\gamma(x,v)\differential\mathbb{\Gamma}_{n}(\gamma)

(125)

\displaystyle\leq\iint\|v\|^{2}_{x}\differential\gamma(x,v)\differential\mathbb{\Gamma}_{n}(\gamma)=\|\mathbb{\Gamma}_{n}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{P}}=\varepsilon_{n}^{2}.

(126)

Thus, we find for every
n>N

\langle\xi,\Phi_{n}-\Psi_{n}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P})}\leq-\frac{\delta}{2}\varepsilon_{n}.

(127)

Now, since
\|\Psi_{n}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P})}\leq\|\mathbb{\Gamma}_{n}\|_{\mathbb{P}}=\varepsilon_{n}
and (by optimality of
\mathbb{\Upsilon}_{n}
)
\|\Phi_{n}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P})}\leq\|\mathbb{\Upsilon}_{n}\|_{\mathbb{P}}=\mathrm{W}_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}(\mathbb{P}_{n},\mathbb{P})\leq\varepsilon_{n}
for every
n
, it ensues that, up to extracting a subsequence, there exists
\Psi,\Phi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{P},T\mathcal{M}))
towards which
\varepsilon_{n}^{-1}\Psi_{n}
and
\varepsilon_{n}^{-1}\Phi_{n}
respectively converge weakly in
L^{2}(\mathbb{P},T\mathcal{M})
. Therefore, dividing (
127
) by
\varepsilon_{n}
and passing to the limit, we find

\langle\xi,\Phi-\Psi\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P})}\leq-\frac{\delta}{2}.

(128)

Now, fix
\mathcal{F}\in\mathrm{Cyl}\big(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})\big)
. By applying
Lemma

C.8
to
\mathbb{\Gamma}_{n}
and
\mathbb{\Upsilon}_{n}
, we find

\displaystyle\int\mathcal{F}\differential\mathbb{P}_{n}

\displaystyle=\int\mathcal{F}\differential\mathbb{P}+\langle\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}\mathcal{F},\Phi_{n}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P})}+O(\varepsilon_{n}^{2}),

(129)

\displaystyle\int\mathcal{F}\differential\mathbb{P}_{n}

\displaystyle=\int\mathcal{F}\differential\mathbb{P}+\langle\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}\mathcal{F},\Psi_{n}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P})}+O(\varepsilon_{n}^{2}).

(130)

Subtracting these two equations, dividing by
\varepsilon_{n}
and passing to the limit, we thus find

\langle\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}\mathcal{F},\Phi-\Psi\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P})}=0,

(131)

and this holds for any
\mathcal{F}\in\mathrm{Cyl}\big(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})\big)
. However, by assumption,
\xi\in T_{\mathbb{P}}\mathcal{P}_{2}\big(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})\big)
, and we recall that

T_{\mathbb{P}}\mathcal{P}_{2}\big(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})\big)=\overline{\{\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F}\in\mathrm{Cyl}\big(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})\big)\}}^{L^{2}(\mathbb{P},T\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})))}.

(132)

This implies immediately that
\langle\xi,\Phi-\Psi\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P})}=0
, which contradicts (
128
). ∎

Lemma C.8
.

Let
\mathcal{F}\in\mathrm{Cyl}\big(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})\big)
, then, for every
\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}\big(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})\big)
and
\mathbb{\Gamma}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}\big(\mathcal{P}_{2}(T\mathcal{M})\big)_{\mathbb{P}}
,

\left|\int\mathcal{F}\differential(\phi^{\exp}_\#\bGamma)-\int\mathcal{F}\differential\mathbb{P}-\iint\langle\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}\mathcal{F}(\pi^{\mathcal{M}}_{\#}\gamma)(x),v\rangle_{x}\differential\gamma(x,v)\differential\mathbb{\Gamma}(\gamma)\right|\leq C\|\mathbb{\Gamma}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{P}}

(133)

for some constant
C
depending only on
\mathcal{F}
.

Proof.

Let
F\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{m})
and
V_{1},\ldots,V_{m}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathcal{M})
be such that

\mathcal{F}(\mu)=F\left(\int V_{1}\differential\mu,\ldots,\int V_{m}\differential\mu\right),\quad\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M}).

(134)

Since
F
is compactly supported, there exists
C>0
which only depends on
F
such that for every
x,h\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
,

\left|F(x+h)-F(x)-\langle\nabla F(x),h\rangle\right|\leq C\|h\|^{2}.

(135)

Fix
\gamma\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(T\mathcal{M})
, and let
\mu:=\pi^{\mathcal{M}}_{\#}\gamma
and
\nu:=\exp_{\#}\gamma
. Since the
V_{i}
are compactly supported, we know by
Lemma

C.12
that there exists some constant
L>0
, which depends only on the
V_{i}
, such that for every
i
,

\left|\int V_{i}\differential\nu-\int V_{i}\differential\mu-\int\langle\nabla V_{i}(x),v\rangle\differential\gamma(x,v)\right|\leq L\|\gamma\|_{\mu}^{2}.

(136)

Now, we have

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{F}(\nu)-\mathcal{F}(\mu)-\int\langle\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}\mathcal{F}(\mu)(x),v\rangle_{x}\differential\gamma(x,v)\right|

(137)

\displaystyle=\left|\mathcal{F}(\nu)-\mathcal{F}(\mu)-\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{\partial F}{\partial x_{i}}\int\langle\nabla V_{i}(x),v\rangle_{x}\differential\gamma(x,v)\right|

(138)

\displaystyle\leq\left|\mathcal{F}(\nu)-\mathcal{F}(\mu)-\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{\partial F}{\partial x_{i}}\left(\int V_{i}\differential\nu-\int V_{i}\differential\mu\right)\right|

(139)

\displaystyle\quad+\left|\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{\partial F}{\partial x_{i}}\left(\int V_{i}\differential\nu-\int V_{i}\differential\mu-\int\langle\nabla V_{i}(x),v\rangle_{x}\differential\gamma(x,v)\right)\right|

(140)

\displaystyle\leq C\sum_{i=1}^{m}\left|\int V_{i}\differential\nu-\int V_{i}\differential\mu\right|^{2}+C\sum_{i=1}^{m}\left|\int V_{i}\differential\nu-\int V_{i}\differential\mu-\int\langle\nabla V_{i}(x),v\rangle\differential\gamma(x,v)\right|

(141)

\displaystyle\leq C\sum_{i=1}^{m}\left|\int V_{i}\differential\nu-\int V_{i}\differential\mu\right|^{2}+C\|\gamma\|^{2}_{\mu}

(142)

\displaystyle\leq C\sum_{i=1}^{m}\left|\int V_{i}\differential\nu-\int V_{i}\differential\mu-\int\langle\nabla V_{i}(x),v\rangle\differential\gamma(x,v)\right|^{2}+\left|\int\langle\nabla V_{i}(x),v\rangle\differential\gamma(x,v)\right|^{2}+C\|\gamma\|^{2}_{\mu}

(143)

\displaystyle\leq C\|\gamma\|^{4}_{\mu}+C\|\gamma\|^{2}_{\mu},

(144)

where we used (
135
) in the fifth line, with
x_{i}=\int V_{i}\differential\mu
and
h_{i}=\int V_{i}\differential\nu-\int V_{i}\differential\mu
, we used (
136
) in the sixth and eighth lines, and we used the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality in the eight line. Throughout the derivation,
C
denotes a constant that may change between lines but which only depends on
F
and the
V_{i}
. In particular, there exists a constant
C
which only depends on
F
and the
V_{i}
such that for every
\gamma\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(T\mathcal{M})
with
\mu=\pi^{\mathcal{M}}_{\#}\gamma
and
\|\gamma\|_{\mu}\leq\mathrm{diam}(\mathcal{M})
,

\left|\mathcal{F}(\exp_{\#}\gamma)-\mathcal{F}(\mu)-\int\langle\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}\mathcal{F}(\mu)(x),v\rangle_{x}\differential\gamma(x,v)\right|\leq C\|\gamma\|^{2}_{\mu}.

(145)

Now, if
\gamma\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(T\mathcal{M})
is such that
\|\gamma\|_{\mu}>\mathrm{diam}(\mathcal{M})
with
\mu:=\pi^{\mathcal{M}}_{\#}\gamma
, let
\nu:=\exp_{\#}\gamma
and
\eta\in\exp_{\mu}^{-1}(\nu)
. Since
\|\eta\|_{\mu}=\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu,\nu)\leq\mathrm{diam}(\mathcal{M})
, this implies

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{F}(\nu)-\mathcal{F}(\mu)-\int\langle\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}\mathcal{F}(\mu)(x),v\rangle_{x}\differential\gamma(x,v)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\mathcal{F}(\nu)-\mathcal{F}(\mu)-\int\langle\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}\mathcal{F}(\mu)(x),v\rangle_{x}\differential\eta(x,v)\right|

(146)

\displaystyle+\left|\int\langle\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}\mathcal{F}(\mu)(x),v\rangle_{x}\differential(\eta-\gamma)(x,v)\right|

(147)

\displaystyle\leq C\|\eta\|_{\mu}^{2}+|\langle\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}\mathcal{F}(\mu),\mathcal{B}(\eta)-\mathcal{B}(\gamma)\rangle_{L^{2}(\mu)}|

(148)

\displaystyle\leq C\|\eta\|_{\mu}^{2}+C(\|\mathcal{B}(\eta)\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}+\|\mathcal{B}(\gamma)\|_{L^{2}(\mu)})

(149)

\displaystyle\leq C(\|\eta\|_{\mu}^{2}+\|\eta\|_{\mu}+\|\gamma\|_{\mu})

(150)

\displaystyle\leq C(\|\gamma\|_{\mu}^{2}+\|\gamma\|_{\mu})

(151)

\displaystyle\leq C\|\gamma\|_{\mu}^{2},

(152)

where we used (
145
) in the third line, and we obtain the fourth line using the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and the fact that
\sup_{\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})}\|\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}\mathcal{F}(\mu)\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}<+\infty
(since the
F
and
V_{i}
are compactly supported). Again the
C
’s denote a constant depending only on
F
and the
V_{i}
(and
\mathrm{diam}(\mathcal{M})
). Thus, we have shown that there exists a constant
C
depending only on
\mathcal{F}
such that for every
\gamma\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(T\mathcal{M})
,

\left|\mathcal{F}(\exp_{\#}\gamma)-\mathcal{F}(\pi^{\mathcal{M}}_{\#}\gamma)-\int\langle\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}\mathcal{F}(\pi^{\mathcal{M}}_{\#}\gamma)(x),v\rangle_{x}\differential\gamma(x,v)\right|\leq C\|\gamma\|^{2}_{\mu}.

(153)

Hence for every
\mathbb{\Gamma}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}\big(\mathcal{P}_{2}(T\mathcal{M})\big)
, noting
\mathbb{P}:=\phi^{\mathcal{M}}_{\#}\mathbb{\Gamma}
and
\mathbb{Q}:=\phi^{\exp}_{\#}\mathbb{\Gamma}
,

\displaystyle\left|\int\mathcal{F}\differential\mathbb{Q}-\int\mathcal{F}\differential\mathbb{P}-\iint\langle\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}\mathcal{F}(\pi^{\mathcal{M}}_{\#}\gamma)(x),v\rangle_{x}\differential\gamma(x,v)\differential\mathbb{\Gamma}(\gamma)\right|

(154)

\displaystyle=\left|\int\mathcal{F}(\exp_{\#}\gamma)-\mathcal{F}(\pi^{\mathcal{M}}_{\#}\gamma)-\langle\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}\mathcal{F}(\pi^{\mathcal{M}}_{\#}\gamma)(x),v\rangle_{x}\differential\gamma(x,v)\differential\mathbb{\Gamma}(\gamma)\right|

(155)

\displaystyle\leq C\int\|\gamma\|^{2}_{\pi^{\mathcal{M}}_{\#}\gamma}\differential\mathbb{\Gamma}(\gamma)=C\|\mathbb{\Gamma}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{P}}.

(156)

This finishes the proof.
∎

C.5
Proof of
Proposition

3.9
, and existence of gradients in the tangent space

First, we prove
Proposition

3.9
. Let
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\partial^{-}\mathbb{F}(\mathbb{P})\cap\partial^{+}\mathbb{F}(\mathbb{P})\cap T_{\mathbb{P}}\mathcal{P}_{2}\big(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})\big)
. Using
Proposition

3.8
, we know that they are also strong gradients of
\mathbb{F}
at
\mathbb{P}
. Therefore, letting
\xi=\xi_{1}-\xi_{2}
, for every
\Psi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{P},T\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M}))
, we have

\int\langle\xi(\mu),\Psi(\mu)\rangle_{L^{2}(\mu)}\differential\mathbb{P}(\mu)=o(\|\Psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P})}).

(157)

that is,
\langle\xi,\Psi\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P})}=o(\|\Psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P})})
. Considering
\Psi=\varepsilon\xi
, we obtain
\varepsilon\|\xi\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P})}=o(\varepsilon)
that is
\|\xi\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{P})}=o(1)
, and this implies
\xi=\xi_{1}-\xi_{2}=0
, and this finishes the proof. ∎

Now, a natural question is to ask whether there is a gradient in
T_{\mathbb{P}}\mathcal{P}_{2}\big(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})\big)
whenever there exists a gradient
\xi\in\partial^{-}\mathbb{F}(\mathbb{P})\cap\partial^{+}\mathbb{F}(\mathbb{P})
. While a complete answer to this question is out of the scope of this article, a partial answer can be provided using results laid out in
(
Schiavo 2020
)
. First, we consider the following assumption:

Assumption C.9
.

(Smooth transport property,
(
Schiavo 2020
, Assumption 2.9)
)
We say that
\mathcal{M}
satisfies the
smooth transport property
if, whenever
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})
are absolutely continuous with smooth nowhere vanishing densities, then there exists a smooth optimal transport map
T:\mathcal{M}\mapsto\mathcal{M}
from
\mu
to
\nu
(in the sense of
Theorem

A.1
).

This is a relatively restrictive assumption on
\mathcal{M}
. By
(
Schiavo 2020
, Theorem 5.9)
, it holds whenever
\mathcal{M}
satisfies the strong Ma-Trudinger-Wang condition
\mathrm{MTW}(K)
for some
K>0
(we refer to
(
Schiavo 2020
, Section 5.2)
for further details). Under this assumption, we can prove the following result on the existence of a gradient in the tangent space:

Proposition C.10
.

Assume that
\mathcal{M}
satisfies
C.9
, and that
\mathbb{P}
satisfies
B.2
. Then, if
\mathbb{F}
admits a WoW gradient at
\mathbb{P}
(i.e.
\partial^{-}\mathbb{F}(\mathbb{P})\cap\partial^{+}\mathbb{F}(\mathbb{P})
is not empty), then it admits a WoW gradient in
T_{\mathbb{P}}\mathcal{P}_{2}\big(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})\big)
(
i.e.
,
\partial^{-}\mathbb{F}(\mathbb{P})\cap\partial^{+}\mathbb{F}(\mathbb{P})\cap T_{\mathbb{P}}\mathcal{P}_{2}\big(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})\big)
is not empty).

Proof.

All we need to do is to prove that for every
\xi\in T_{\mathbb{P}}\mathcal{P}_{2}\big(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})\big)^{\perp}
,
\mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}\big(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})\big)
and
\mathbb{\Gamma}\in\exp_{\mathbb{P}}^{-1}(\mathbb{Q})
,

\iint\langle\xi(\pi^{\mathcal{M}}_{\#}\gamma)(x),v\rangle_{x}\differential\gamma(x,v)\differential\mathbb{\Gamma}(\gamma)=0.

(158)

Indeed, this ensures that if
\xi\in\partial^{-}\mathbb{F}(\mathbb{P})\cap\partial^{+}\mathbb{F}(\mathbb{P})
is a WoW gradient of
\mathbb{F}
at
\mathbb{P}
, then its orthogonal projection on
T_{\mathbb{P}}\mathcal{P}_{2}\big(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})\big)
is also a WoW gradient.

We thus fix
\xi\in T_{\mathbb{P}}\mathcal{P}_{2}\big(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})\big)^{\perp}
,
\mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}\big(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})\big)
and
\mathbb{\Gamma}\in\exp_{\mathbb{P}}^{-1}(\mathbb{Q})
. Since
\mathbb{P}
satisfies
B.2
, by
Remark

C.4
,
\mathbb{\Gamma}
is of the form
(\mu\mapsto(\mathrm{Id},-D_{\mathbb{P}}U)_{\#}\mu)_{\#}\mathbb{P}
where
U
is a Kantorovich potential for the pair
\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q}
. However, since
\mathcal{M}
satisfies
C.9
, and
U
is
\mathrm{W}_{2}
-Lipschitz (by
(
Emami & Pass 2025
, Lemma 12)
),
(
Schiavo 2020
, Theorem 2.10(3))
implies that
D_{\mathbb{P}}U\in T_{\mathbb{P}}\mathcal{P}_{2}\big(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})\big)
(as a limit in
L^{2}(\mathbb{P},T\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M}))
of functions of the form
\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}\mathcal{F}
,
\mathcal{F}\in\mathrm{Cyl}\big(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})\big)
), so that

\iint\langle\xi(\pi^{\mathcal{M}}_{\#}\gamma)(x),v\rangle_{x}\differential\gamma(x,v)\differential\mathbb{\Gamma}(\gamma)=-\int\langle\xi(\mu),D_{\mathbb{P}}U(\mu)\rangle_{L^{2}(\mu)}\differential\mathbb{P}(\mu)=0.

(159)

This finishes the proof.
∎

Note that for this proposition to hold,
\mathbb{P}
must not simply be absolutely continuous w.r.t
\mathbb{P}_{0}
, but must itself satisfy
B.2
. According to
(
Schiavo 2020
, Proposition 5.2)
, this is the case whenever, for instance,
\mathbb{P}=\varphi^{2}\mathbb{P}_{0}
where
\varphi
is a strictly positive
\mathrm{W}_{2}
-Lipschitz function on
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})
.

C.6
Proof of
Proposition

4.1

We aim at applying
(
Ambrosio et al. 2008
, Theorem 4.0.4)
. Since by hypothesis,
\mathbb{F}
is
\lambda
-convex along the curve
\mathbb{P}_{t}=\big(\big((1-t)\mathrm{T}_{\pi^{1}}^{\pi^{2}}+t\mathrm{T}_{\pi^{1}}^{\pi^{3}}\big)_{\#}\pi^{1}\big)_{\#}\Gamma
for
\Gamma\in\Pi(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{O})
and
\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}\big(\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{2,ac}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)
, we need to show that
\mathbb{G}:\mathbb{Q}\mapsto\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{W}_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}(\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{P})^{2}
is 1-convex along
\mathbb{P}_{t}
(see
e.g.

(
Ambrosio et al. 2008
, Lemma 9.2.7)
). This is well the case by
Proposition

B.10
.

Now, let
\mathbb{P}_{k}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}\big(\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{2,ac}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)
and
\mathbb{J}(\mathbb{P})=\frac{1}{2\tau}\mathrm{W}_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{P}_{k})^{2}+\mathbb{F}(\mathbb{P})
the functional solved at each step of the JKO scheme. Then, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{J}(\mathbb{P}_{t})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\tau}\mathrm{W}_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}(\mathbb{P}_{t},\mathbb{P}_{k})^{2}+\mathbb{F}(\mathbb{P}_{t})

(160)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\tau}\mathbb{G}(\mathbb{P})+\mathbb{F}(\mathbb{P})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\tau}\big((1-t)\mathbb{G}(\mathbb{Q})+t\mathbb{G}(\mathbb{O})-\frac{t(1-t)}{2}\mathrm{W}_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}(\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{O})^{2}\big)

\displaystyle+(1-t)\mathbb{F}(\mathbb{Q})+t\mathbb{F}(\mathbb{O})-\frac{\lambda t(1-t)}{2}\mathrm{W}_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}(\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{O})^{2}

\displaystyle=(1-t)\mathbb{J}(\mathbb{Q})+t\mathbb{J}(\mathbb{O})-\frac{\lambda\tau+1}{2\tau}t(1-t)\mathrm{W}_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}(\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{O})^{2}.

Thus, we conclude that
\mathbb{J}
satisfies well
(
Ambrosio et al. 2008
, Assumption (4.0.1))
, and then apply
(
Ambrosio et al. 2008
, Theorem 4.0.4)
. ∎

C.7
Proof of
Proposition

A.4

Let
\xi,\xi^{\prime}\in T_{\mu}\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})\cap\partial^{+}\mathcal{F}(\mu)\cap\partial^{-}\mathcal{F}(\mu)
. By density, for any
\varepsilon>0
, there exist
\varphi_{\varepsilon},\varphi_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathcal{M})
such that
\|\xi-\nabla\varphi_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\mu,T\mathcal{M})}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
and
\|\xi^{\prime}-\nabla\varphi^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}(\mu,T\mathcal{M})}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
.

We rely on the following Lemma, which provides an OT map for any
\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathcal{M})
for
s
small enough.

Lemma C.11
.

Let
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})
and
\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathcal{M})
. Then, there exists
\bar{s}
such that
x\mapsto\exp_{x}(s\nabla\psi(x))
is an OT map between
\mu
and
\big(\exp\circ(s\nabla\psi)\big)_{\#}\mu
for all
s\in]-\bar{s},\bar{s}[
.

Proof.

Suppose
\psi\neq 0
. Let
\varepsilon>0
and
\bar{s}=\frac{\varepsilon}{\max_{x}\|\nabla^{2}\psi(x)\|}
. It exists as
\psi
is supported on a compact. Then, for any
s\in(-\bar{s},\bar{s})
,
\|s\nabla^{2}\psi\|\leq\bar{s}\|\nabla^{2}\psi\|\leq\varepsilon
. Then, by
(
Villani et al. 2009
, Theorem 13.5)
,
s\psi
is
d^{2}/2
convex, and by McCann’s theorem,
\exp(s\nabla\psi)
is an OT map.
∎

By
Lemma

C.11
, there exists
s>0
such that
\gamma=(\mathrm{Id},s\nabla\varphi_{\varepsilon})_{\#}\mu\in\exp_{\mu}^{-1}(\nu)
and
\gamma^{\prime}=(\mathrm{Id},s\nabla\varphi_{\varepsilon}^{\prime})_{\#}\mu\in\exp_{\mu}^{-1}(\nu^{\prime})
with
\nu=\big(\exp\circ(s\nabla\varphi_{\varepsilon})\big)_{\#}\mu
and
\nu^{\prime}=\big(\exp\circ(s\nabla\varphi_{\varepsilon}^{\prime})\big)_{\#}\mu
. Thus, we have using the definitions of sub-differentials that

\begin{cases}\mathcal{F}(\nu)\geq\mathcal{F}(\mu)+s\int\langle\xi(x),\nabla\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x)\rangle_{x}\ \mathrm{d}\mu(x)+o(s)\\
\mathcal{F}(\nu^{\prime})\geq\mathcal{F}(\mu)+s\int\langle\xi^{\prime}(x),\nabla\varphi_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(x)\rangle_{x}\ \mathrm{d}\mu(x)+o(s).\end{cases}

(161)

Likewise, by the definition of the super-differentials, we have

\begin{cases}\mathcal{F}(\nu^{\prime})\leq\mathcal{F}(\mu)+s\int\langle\xi(x),\nabla\varphi_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(x)\rangle_{x}\ \mathrm{d}\mu(x)+o(s)\\
\mathcal{F}(\nu)\leq\mathcal{F}(\mu)+s\int\langle\xi^{\prime}(x),\nabla\varphi_{\varepsilon}(x)\rangle_{x}\ \mathrm{d}\mu(x)+o(s).\end{cases}

(162)

Dividing by
s>0
and rearranging the terms, we have

\begin{cases}\frac{\mathcal{F}(\nu)-\mathcal{F}(\mu)}{s}\geq\langle\xi,\nabla\varphi_{\varepsilon}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mu,T\mathcal{M})}+o(1)\\
\frac{\mathcal{F}(\nu^{\prime})-\mathcal{F}(\mu)}{s}\geq\langle\xi^{\prime},\nabla\varphi_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mu,T\mathcal{M})}+o(1)\\
\frac{\mathcal{F}(\mu)-\mathcal{F}(\nu^{\prime})}{s}\geq\langle-\xi,\nabla\varphi_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mu,T\mathcal{M})}+o(1)\\
\frac{\mathcal{F}(\mu)-\mathcal{F}(\nu)}{s}\geq\langle-\xi^{\prime},\nabla\varphi_{\varepsilon}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mu,T\mathcal{M})}+o(1).\end{cases}

(163)

Summing them, we get,

0\geq\langle\xi-\xi^{\prime},\nabla\varphi_{\varepsilon}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mu,T\mathcal{M})}+\langle\xi^{\prime}-\xi,\nabla\varphi_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mu,T\mathcal{M})}+o(1)=\langle\xi-\xi^{\prime},\nabla\varphi_{\varepsilon}-\nabla\varphi_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mu,T\mathcal{M})}+o(1).

(164)

Then, we have

\displaystyle\|\xi-\xi^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}(\mu,T\mathcal{M})}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\|\xi-\xi^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}(\mu,T\mathcal{M})}^{2}-2\langle\xi-\xi^{\prime},\nabla\varphi_{\varepsilon}-\nabla\varphi_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mu,T\mathcal{M})}+\|\nabla\varphi_{\varepsilon}-\nabla\varphi_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}(\mu,T\mathcal{M})}^{2}}

(165)

\displaystyle=\|\xi-\xi^{\prime}-(\nabla\varphi_{\varepsilon}-\nabla\varphi_{\varepsilon}^{\prime})\|_{L^{2}(\mu,T\mathcal{M})}

\displaystyle\leq\|\xi-\nabla\varphi_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\mu,T\mathcal{M})}+\|\xi^{\prime}-\nabla\varphi_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}(\mu,T\mathcal{M})}

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon.

Taking the limit
\varepsilon\to 0
, we conclude that
\xi=\xi^{\prime}
. ∎

C.8
Proof of
Proposition

A.5

We assume by contradiction that
\xi
is not an extended strong subdifferential. Then there exists a sequence
\{\gamma_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{2}(T\mathcal{M})_{\mu}
and
\delta>0
such that
\varepsilon_{n}:=\|\gamma_{n}\|_{\mu}\to 0
and for every
n
, denoting
\mu_{n}:=\exp_{\#}\gamma_{n}
,

\mathcal{F}(\mu_{n})-\mathcal{F}(\mu)-\int\langle\xi(x),v\rangle_{x}\differential\gamma_{n}(x,v)\leq-\delta\varepsilon_{n}.

(166)

Now, let
\eta_{n}\in\exp^{-1}_{\mu}(\mu_{n})
. Since
\xi
is a subdifferential, there exists
N
such that for every
n>N
,

\mathcal{F}(\mu_{n})-\mathcal{F}(\mu)-\int\langle\xi(x),v\rangle_{x}\differential\eta_{n}(x,v)\geq-\frac{\delta}{2}\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu,\mu_{n}).

(167)

Since, by optimality of
\eta_{n}
, we have
\|\eta_{n}\|_{\mu}=\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu,\mu_{n})\leq\|\gamma_{n}\|_{\mu}=\varepsilon_{n}
, combining these inequalities, we find

\int\langle\xi(x),v\rangle_{x}\differential\eta_{n}(x,v)-\int\langle\xi(x),v\rangle_{x}\differential\gamma_{n}(x,v)\leq-\frac{\delta}{2}\varepsilon_{n}

(168)

that is

\langle\xi,\Phi_{n}-\Psi_{n}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leq-\frac{\delta}{2}\varepsilon_{n}

(169)

for every
n>N
, where we have defined
\Psi_{n}:=\mathcal{B}(\gamma_{n})
and
\Phi_{n}:=\mathcal{B}(\eta_{n})
.
Since we have
\|\Psi_{n}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leq\|\gamma_{n}\|_{\mu}=\varepsilon_{n}
and likewise
\|\Phi_{n}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leq\|\eta_{n}\|_{\mu}\leq\varepsilon_{n}
, up to extracting a subsequence we can assume that there exists
\Psi,\Phi\in L^{2}(\mu,T\mathcal{M})
towards which
\varepsilon_{n}^{-1}\Psi_{n}
and
\varepsilon_{n}^{-1}\Phi_{n}
respectively converge weakly in
L^{2}(\mu,T\mathcal{M})
. Thus, dividing (
169
) by
\varepsilon_{n}
and passing to the limit, we find

\langle\xi,\Phi-\Psi\rangle_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leq-\frac{\delta}{2}.

(170)

Now, fix some
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathcal{M})
. By
Lemma

C.12
, we have

\displaystyle\int\varphi\differential\mu_{n}

\displaystyle=\int\varphi\differential\mu+\int\langle\nabla\varphi(x),v\rangle_{x}\differential\eta_{n}(x,v)+O(\|\eta_{n}\|^{2}_{\mu})

(171)

\displaystyle=\int\varphi\differential\mu+\langle\nabla\varphi,\Phi_{n}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mu)}+O(\varepsilon_{n}^{2}),

(172)

and similarly

\int\varphi\differential\mu_{n}=\int\varphi\differential\mu+\langle\nabla\varphi,\Psi_{n}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mu)}+O(\varepsilon_{n}^{2}).

(173)

Subtracting these two equations, dividing by
\varepsilon_{n}
and passing to the limit, we find

\langle\nabla\varphi,\Phi-\Psi\rangle_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0

(174)

and this holds for any
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathcal{M})
. However, by assumption,
\xi\in T_{\mu}\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})=\overline{\{\nabla\varphi,\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathcal{M})\}}^{L^{2}(\mu,T\mathcal{M})}
. This implies immediately that
\langle\xi,\Phi-\Psi\rangle_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0
, which contradicts (
170
). ∎

Lemma C.12
.

Let
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathcal{M})
, then, for every
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})
and
\gamma\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(T\mathcal{M})_{\mu}
,

\left|\int\varphi\differential(\exp_\#\gamma)-\int\varphi\differential\mu-\int\langle\nabla\varphi(x),v\rangle_{x}\differential\gamma(x,v)\right|\leq\frac{1}{2}L\|\gamma\|^{2}_{\mu}

(175)

where
L:=\max_{(x,v)\in T\mathcal{M},\|v\|_{x}=1}\|\Hess_{\mathcal{M}}\varphi(x)[v]\|<\infty
.

Proof.

Let
(x,v)\in T\mathcal{M}
. Applying
(
Boumal 2023
, Exercise 5.40)
to the geodesic given by
c(t)=\exp_{x}(tv)
, it ensues that there exists
t\in(0,1)
such that

\varphi(\exp_{x}(v))=\varphi(x)+\langle\nabla\varphi(x),v\rangle_{x}+\frac{1}{2}\langle\Hess\varphi(c(t))[c^{\prime}(t)],c^{\prime}(t)\rangle_{c(t)}

(176)

so that, since
\|c^{\prime}(t)\|_{c(t)}=\|v\|_{x}
,

\left|\varphi(\exp_{x}(v))-\varphi(x)-\langle\nabla\varphi(x),v\rangle_{x}\right|\leq\frac{1}{2}L\|v\|^{2}_{x}.

(177)

This immediately implies

\displaystyle\left|\int\varphi\differential(\exp_\#\gamma)-\int\varphi\differential\mu-\int\langle\nabla\varphi(x),v\rangle\differential\gamma(x,v)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\int\varphi(\exp_{x}(v))-\varphi(x)-\langle\nabla\varphi(x),v\rangle\differential\gamma(x,v)\right|

(178)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}L\int\|v\|^{2}_{x}\differential\gamma(x,v)=\frac{1}{2}L\|\gamma\|^{2}_{\mu}.

(179)

∎

C.9
Proof of
Proposition

A.6

Let
\nu,\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})
, and
\gamma\in\exp_{\mu}^{-1}(\nu)
. For any
x\in\mathcal{M}
,
v\in T_{x}\mathcal{M}
, let us note
c_{x,v}(t)=\exp_{x}(tv)
the geodesic starting from
x
with direction
v
. By
(
Boumal 2023
, Exercise 5.40)
, we have that there exists
t\in[0,1]
such that

V\big(\exp_{x}(v)\big)=V(x)+\langle\nabla_{\mathcal{M}}V(x),v\rangle_{x}+\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathrm{Hess}V\big(c_{x,v}(t)\big)[c_{x,v}^{\prime}(t)],c_{x,v}^{\prime}(t)\rangle_{c_{x,v}(t)}.

(180)

Then,

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}(\nu)-\mathcal{V}(\mu)

\displaystyle=\int\big(V(\exp_{x}(v))-V(x)\big)\ \mathrm{d}\gamma(x,v)

(181)

\displaystyle=\int\langle\nabla_{\mathcal{M}}V(x),v\rangle_{x}+\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathrm{Hess}V\big(c_{x,v}(t)\big)[c_{x,v}^{\prime}(t)],c_{x,v}^{\prime}(t)\rangle_{c_{x,v}(t)}\ \mathrm{d}\gamma(x,v)

\displaystyle=\int\langle\nabla_{\mathcal{M}}V(x),v\rangle_{x}\ \mathrm{d}\gamma(x,v)+\frac{1}{2}\int\langle\mathrm{Hess}V\big(c_{x,v}(t)\big)[c_{x,v}^{\prime}(t)],c_{x,v}^{\prime}(t)\rangle_{c_{x,v}(t)}\ \mathrm{d}\gamma(x,v).

Moreover, using that
V
has bounded Hessian and that geodesics are constant speed and thus satisfy
\|c_{x,v}^{\prime}(t)\|_{c_{x,v}(t)}=\|c_{x,v}^{\prime}(0)\|_{c_{x,v}(0)}=\|v\|_{x}

(
Lee 2006
, Lemma 5.5)
, we have that the last term is bounded by
\mathrm{W}_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu)
as

\displaystyle\left|\int\langle\mathrm{Hess}V\big(c_{x,v}(t)\big)[c_{x,v}^{\prime}(t)],c_{x,v}^{\prime}(t)\rangle_{c_{x,v}(t)}\ \mathrm{d}\gamma(x,v)\right|

\displaystyle\leq L\int\|c_{x,v}^{\prime}(t)\|_{c_{x,v}(t)}^{2}\ \mathrm{d}\gamma(x,v)

(182)

\displaystyle=L\int\|v\|_{x}^{2}\ \mathrm{d}\gamma(x,v)=L\mathrm{W}_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu).

Thus, we conclude

\mathcal{V}(\nu)=\mathcal{V}(\mu)+\int\langle\nabla_{\mathcal{M}}V(x),v\rangle_{x}\ \mathrm{d}\gamma(x,v)+o\big(\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu,\nu)\big).

(183)

Now, let us verify that
\nabla_{\mathcal{M}}V\in L^{2}(\mu)
. We denote by
\mathrm{PT}_{x\to y}
the parallel transport between
T_{x}\mathcal{M}
and
T_{y}\mathcal{M}
along the geodesic between
x
and
y
(see
(
Boumal 2023
, Definition 10.35)
for the definition).
By
(
Boumal 2023
, Corollary 10.48, 3.)
,
V
having its Hessian bounded in operator norm by
L
is equivalent with having for all
x,y\in\mathcal{M}
, for all
(x,v)\in T\mathcal{M}
,

\|\nabla_{\mathcal{M}}V(x)-\mathrm{PT}_{\exp_{x}(v)\to x}\nabla_{\mathcal{M}}V\big(\exp_{x}(v)\big)\|_{x}\leq L\|v\|_{x}.

(184)

Thus, let
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})
,
o
some origin, and
\gamma\in\exp_{\mu}^{-1}(\delta_{o})
. Then, we have by using sequentially the definition of
\gamma
,
\|x+y\|^{2}\leq 2\|x\|^{2}+2\|y\|^{2}
, (
184
), that for any
(x,v)\in\mathrm{supp}(\gamma)
,
\exp_{x}(v)=o
and
\mathrm{PT}_{o\to x}
is an isometry
(
Boumal 2023
, Proposition 10.36)
, and
\gamma\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(T\mathcal{M})
,

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\mathcal{M}}V\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int\|\nabla_{\mathcal{M}}V(x)\|_{x}^{2}\ \mathrm{d}\mu(x)

(185)

\displaystyle=\int\|\nabla_{\mathcal{M}}V(x)\|_{x}^{2}\ \mathrm{d}\gamma(x,v)

\displaystyle\leq 2\int\|\nabla_{\mathcal{M}}V(x)-\mathrm{PT}_{\exp_{x}(v)\to x}\nabla_{\mathcal{M}}V\big(\exp_{x}(v)\big)\|_{x}^{2}\ \mathrm{d}\gamma(x,v)

\displaystyle+2\int\|\mathrm{PT}_{\exp_{x}(v)\to x}\nabla_{\mathcal{M}}V\big(\exp_{x}(v)\big)\|_{x}^{2}\ \mathrm{d}\gamma(x,v)

\displaystyle\leq 2\int L\|v\|_{x}^{2}\ \mathrm{d}\gamma(x,v)+2\|\nabla_{\mathcal{M}}V(o)\|_{o}^{2}

\displaystyle<+\infty.

Therefore, we can conclude that
\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}\mathcal{V}(\mu)=\nabla_{\mathcal{M}}V
by
Definition

A.3
. ∎

C.10
Proof of
Proposition

A.7

Let
\nu,\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathcal{M})
, and
\gamma\in\exp_{\mu}^{-1}(\nu)
. First, we recall that the product space
\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M}
is a Riemannian manifold with tangent space
T\mathcal{M}\times T\mathcal{M}
. For any
(x,v),(x^{\prime},v^{\prime})\in T\mathcal{M}
and note
c_{x}(t)=\exp_{x}(tv)
,
c_{x^{\prime},v^{\prime}}(t)=\exp_{x^{\prime}}(tv^{\prime})
and
c_{x,x^{\prime},v,v^{\prime}}(t)=(c_{x,v}(t),c_{x^{\prime},v^{\prime}}(t))
the geodesics starting at
(x,x^{\prime})
with direction
(v,v^{\prime})
. Then, by
(
Boumal 2023
, Exercise 5.40)
, there exists
t\in]0,1[
such that

\displaystyle W\big(\exp_{x}(v),\exp_{x^{\prime}}(v^{\prime})\big)

\displaystyle=W(x,x^{\prime})+\langle\nabla_{1}W(x,x^{\prime}),v\rangle_{x}+\langle\nabla_{2}W(x,x^{\prime}),v^{\prime}\rangle_{x^{\prime}}

(186)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathrm{Hess}W(c_{x,v}(t),c_{x^{\prime},v^{\prime}}(t))[c_{x,v}^{\prime}(t),c_{x^{\prime},v^{\prime}}^{\prime}(t)],[c_{x,v}^{\prime}(t),c_{x^{\prime},v^{\prime}}^{\prime}(t)]\rangle_{c_{x,x^{\prime},v,v^{\prime}}(t)}.

Moreover, by the same argument as (
182
) in
Proposition

A.6
, we have

\left|\iint\langle\mathrm{Hess}W(c_{x,v,x^{\prime},v^{\prime}}(t))[c_{x,v}^{\prime}(t),c_{x^{\prime},v^{\prime}}^{\prime}(t)],[c_{x,v}^{\prime}(t),c_{x^{\prime},v^{\prime}}^{\prime}(t)]\rangle_{c_{x,x^{\prime},v,v^{\prime}}(t)}\ \mathrm{d}\gamma(x,v)\mathrm{d}\gamma(x^{\prime},v^{\prime})\right|\leq 2L\mathrm{W}_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu).

(187)

Then, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}(\nu)-\mathcal{W}(\mu)

\displaystyle=\iint W(y,y^{\prime})\ \mathrm{d}\nu(y)\mathrm{d}\nu(y^{\prime})-\iint W(x,x^{\prime})\ \mathrm{d}\mu(x)\mathrm{d}\mu(x^{\prime})

(188)

\displaystyle=\iint\big(W(\exp_{x}(v),\exp_{x^{\prime}}(v^{\prime}))-W(x,x^{\prime})\big)\ \mathrm{d}\gamma(x,v)\mathrm{d}\gamma(x^{\prime},v^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int\big(\langle\nabla_{1}W(x,x^{\prime}),v\rangle_{x}+\langle\nabla_{2}W(x,x^{\prime}),v^{\prime}\rangle_{x^{\prime}}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathrm{Hess}W(c_{x,v}(t),c_{x^{\prime},v^{\prime}}(t))[c_{x,v}^{\prime}(t),c_{x^{\prime},v^{\prime}}(t)],[c_{x,v}^{\prime}(t),c_{x^{\prime},v^{\prime}}(t^{\prime})]\rangle_{c_{x,x^{\prime},v,v^{\prime}}(t)}\big)\ \mathrm{d}\gamma(x,v)\mathrm{d}\gamma(x^{\prime},v^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int\langle\int\nabla_{1}W(x,x^{\prime})\mathrm{d}\mu(x^{\prime}),v\rangle_{x}\ \mathrm{d}\gamma(x,v)+\int\langle\int\nabla_{2}W(x,x^{\prime})\mathrm{d}\mu(x),v^{\prime}\rangle_{x^{\prime}}\ \mathrm{d}\gamma(x^{\prime},v^{\prime})+o\big(\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu,\nu)\big)

\displaystyle=\int\left\langle\int\big(\nabla_{1}W(x,x^{\prime})+\nabla_{2}W(x^{\prime},x)\big)\ \mathrm{d}\mu(x^{\prime}),v\ \right\rangle_{x}\ \mathrm{d}\gamma(x,v)+o\big(\mathrm{W}_{2}(\mu,\nu)\big).

Now, let
\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}\mathcal{W}(\mu)=\int\big(\nabla_{1}W(\cdot,x)+\nabla_{2}(x,\cdot)\big)\ \mathrm{d}\mu(x)
. Using that by Jensen’s inequality,

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}\mathcal{W}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int\left\|\int\big(\nabla_{1}W(x,x^{\prime})+\nabla_{2}W(x^{\prime},x)\big)\ \mathrm{d}\mu(x^{\prime})\right\|_{x}^{2}\ \mathrm{d}\mu(x)

(189)

\displaystyle\leq 2\iint\big(\|\nabla_{1}W(x,x^{\prime})\|_{x}^{2}+\|\nabla_{2}W(x,x^{\prime})\|_{x^{\prime}}^{2}\big)\ \mathrm{d}\mu(x)\mathrm{d}\mu(x^{\prime}),

and a similar reasoning of (
185
), we find that
\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}\mathcal{W}\in L^{2}(\mu)
, and we can conclude that
\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}\mathcal{W}
is a Wasserstein gradient by
Definition

A.3
. ∎

C.11
Proof of
Proposition

A.9

Let
\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\mu_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{x_{i}}
. Since
\mathcal{F}
is Wasserstein differentiable, the Wasserstein gradient
\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}\mathcal{F}(\mu_{n})
satisfies for any coupling
\gamma\in\Pi(\mu_{n},\nu)

(
Lanzetti et al. 2025
, Proposition 2.12)
,

\mathcal{F}(\nu)=\mathcal{F}(\mu_{n})+\int\langle\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}\mathcal{F}(\mu_{n})(x),y-x\rangle\ \mathrm{d}\gamma(x,y)+o\left(\sqrt{\int\|x-y\|_{2}^{2}\ \mathrm{d}\gamma(x,y)}\right).

(190)

Let
h_{1},\dots,h_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\nu_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{x_{i}+h_{i}}
and
\gamma_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{(x_{i},x_{i}+h_{i})}\in\Pi(\mu_{n},\nu_{n})
. Then, since
F(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=\mathcal{F}(\mu_{n})
and
F(x_{1}+h_{1},\dots,x_{n}+h_{n})=\mathcal{F}(\nu_{n})
, we get

\displaystyle F(x_{1}+h_{1},\dots,x_{n}+h_{n})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}(\nu_{n})

(191)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}(\mu_{n})+\int\langle\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}\mathcal{F}(\mu_{n})(x),y-x\rangle\ \mathrm{d}\gamma_{n}(x,y)+o\left(\sqrt{\int\|x-y\|_{2}^{2}\ \mathrm{d}\gamma_{n}(x,y)}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}(\mu_{n})+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\langle\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}\mathcal{F}(\mu_{n})(x_{i}),h_{i}\rangle+o\left(\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|h_{i}\|_{2}^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=F(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big\langle\frac{1}{n}\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}\mathcal{F}(\mu_{n})(x_{i}),h_{i}\big\rangle+o\left(\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|h_{i}\|_{2}^{2}}\right).

Thus, by definition of the gradient of
F
, we deduce that
\nabla_{i}F(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=\frac{1}{n}\nabla_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}\mathcal{F}(\mu_{n})(x_{i})
. ∎

C.12
Proof of
Proposition

B.4

Let
\mathbb{\Gamma}\in\exp_{\mathbb{P}}^{-1}(\mathbb{Q})
. Let
s,t\in[0,1]
, and
\phi^{s}(\gamma)=(\exp_{\pi^{\mathcal{M}}}\circ(s\pi^{\mathrm{v}}))_{\#}\gamma
for
\gamma\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(T\mathcal{M})
. Then, (
\phi^{s},\phi^{t})_{\#}\mathbb{\Gamma}\in\Pi(\mathbb{P}_{s},\mathbb{P}_{t})
. Therefore,

\mathrm{W}_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}(\mathbb{P}_{s},\mathbb{P}_{t})^{2}\leq\int\mathrm{W}_{2}^{2}\big(\phi^{s}(\gamma),\phi^{t}(\gamma)\big)\ \mathrm{d}\mathbb{\Gamma}(\gamma).

(192)

Moreover, since for
\mathbb{\Gamma}
-a.e.
\gamma
,
\big(\exp_{\pi^{\mathcal{M}}}\circ(s\pi^{\mathrm{v}}),\exp_{\pi^{\mathcal{M}}}\circ(t\pi^{\mathrm{v}})\big)_{\#}\gamma\in\Pi(\phi^{s}(\gamma),\phi^{t}(\gamma))
, we have the following inequality:

\displaystyle\mathrm{W}_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}(\mathbb{P}_{s},\mathbb{P}_{t})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\int\mathrm{W}_{2}^{2}\big(\phi^{s}(\gamma),\phi^{t}(\gamma)\big)\ \mathrm{d}\mathbb{\Gamma}(\gamma)

(193)

\displaystyle\leq\iint d\big(\exp_{x}(sv),\exp_{x}(tv)\big)^{2}\ \mathrm{d}\gamma(x,v)\mathrm{d}\mathbb{\Gamma}(\gamma)

\displaystyle=|t-s|^{2}\iint\|v\|_{x}^{2}\ \mathrm{d}\gamma(x,v)\mathrm{d}\mathbb{\Gamma}(\gamma)

\displaystyle=|t-s|^{2}\mathrm{W}_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})^{2},

where we used that
\mathbb{\Gamma}\in\exp_{\mathbb{P}}^{-1}(\mathbb{Q})
and that
d\big(\exp_{x}(tv),\exp_{x}(sv)\big)=|t-s|\|v\|_{x}
.

For the other inequality, we have for any
0\leq s<t\leq 1
, using the triangle inequality and the previous inequality,

\displaystyle\mathrm{W}_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{W}_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{P}_{s})+\mathrm{W}_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}(\mathbb{P}_{s},\mathbb{P}_{t})+\mathrm{W}_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}(\mathbb{P}_{t},\mathbb{Q})

(194)

\displaystyle\leq s\mathrm{W}_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})+\mathrm{W}_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}(\mathbb{P}_{s},\mathbb{P}_{t})+(1-t)\mathrm{W}_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q}).

This is equivalent with

(t-s)\mathrm{W}_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})\leq\mathrm{W}_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}(\mathbb{P}_{s},\mathbb{P}_{t}).

(195)

Thus, we can conclude that
\mathrm{W}_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}(\mathbb{P}_{s},\mathbb{P}_{t})=|t-s|\mathrm{W}_{\mathrm{W}_{2}}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})
and thus
t\mapsto\mathbb{P}_{t}
is a constant-speed geodesic between
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
. ∎

C.13
Proof of
Proposition

B.5

We first state a lemma showing a relation between
\gamma\in\exp_{\mu}^{-1}(\nu)
and a specifically constructed
